\chapter*{Medical disclaimer}
\phantomsection
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Medical disclaimer}
This book is an educational reference, not a substitute for a clinical examination, diagnosis, or individualized medical advice. It does not tell a reader whether to start, stop, or change a medicine, vaccine, supplement, diet, or treatment. Urgent symptoms require prompt local medical care.
\chapter*{Preface}
\phantomsection
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Preface}
Medical myths are more than harmless pieces of folklore. Some are repeated so often that they shape decisions about medicines, vaccines, pregnancy, food, emergencies, and when to seek care. Others begin with a genuine observation and then attach an explanation that the evidence does not support. This book examines those claims carefully and separates what is established from what is uncertain.

The book is organized into two main collections. \emph{Common Medical Myths} covers widely circulated health claims found across many countries and communities. \emph{Indian Myths} focuses on beliefs associated with particular regions, families, and social or traditional practices in India. The second collection is not a description of all Indians or Hindus. Beliefs vary widely, and cultural meaning should be discussed with respect even when a medical claim is unsupported.

Each entry follows the same simple pattern: Myth, Status, and Fact. ``False'' means that the claim is not supported as stated. ``True'' means that the claim is supported within the circumstances described. ``Uncertain'' means that evidence is limited, mixed, or dependent on context. These labels describe the claim, not the worth of the person who believes it.

The explanations aim to be useful rather than merely dismissive. They identify the relevant biology, clarify important exceptions, point out possible harms from delay or inappropriate treatment, and direct readers toward appropriate professional care when necessary. The bibliography provides the evidence base used for the collection, but medical knowledge changes; readers should confirm current recommendations with qualified clinicians and relevant public-health or professional organizations.

This is an educational reference, not a substitute for examination, diagnosis, or individualized medical advice. No entry should be used by itself to start, stop, or change a medicine, vaccine, supplement, diet, or treatment. In an emergency, seek local emergency care first.

\chapter{Common Medical Myths}
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=0pt,labelwidth=0pt,labelsep=0pt,itemindent=0pt]
\item[] \textbf{GEN-001.} \textbf{Myth:} You need exactly 8 glasses of water every day.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Water needs vary with activity, climate, diet, and health. Plain water, other beverages, and moisture in food all contribute to daily intake. For most healthy adults under ordinary conditions, thirst is a reasonable guide. Heat and prolonged exercise can increase fluid needs. \cite{ref292}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} There is no need to force yourself to finish an eighth glass or to stop at eight if you are still thirsty. If your clinician has prescribed a fluid target, follow that individualized advice.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-002.} \textbf{Myth:} Going outside with wet hair causes a cold.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} A common cold is an infection caused by a respiratory virus, often a rhinovirus. Going outside with wet hair cannot by itself cause that infection. \cite{ref4} In a controlled study of volunteers exposed to rhinovirus, researchers found no clear evidence that chilling increased their likelihood of developing a cold. The study examined exposure to cold air and did not directly test wet hair. \cite{ref239}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Drying your hair before going outdoors is a matter of comfort. To reduce the chance of catching or spreading a cold, wash your hands regularly and improve ventilation in shared indoor spaces. \cite{ref4}
\item[] \textbf{GEN-003.} \textbf{Myth:} Cold weather itself causes the common cold.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} A cold develops after infection with a respiratory virus. Some of these viruses circulate more widely in colder months, but people can catch colds at any time of year.\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Cold weather alone cannot give you a cold. Washing your hands and bringing fresh air into shared indoor spaces can help reduce exposure to respiratory viruses. \cite{ref4}
\item[] \textbf{GEN-004.} \textbf{Myth:} Green mucus proves you have a bacterial infection.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Nasal mucus can turn yellow or green during an ordinary viral cold. Color alone cannot identify the cause of the illness or show that an antibiotic would help.\newline
\textbf{Implications:} How symptoms change over time is more informative. Seek medical advice if sinus symptoms persist for at least ten days without improvement, become severe, or worsen after initially improving. \cite{ref294,ref241}
\item[] \textbf{GEN-005.} \textbf{Myth:} You should ``sweat out'' a fever.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Sweating often begins naturally as a fever subsides. Wrapping up in heavy blankets to induce sweating traps heat and can increase discomfort and fluid loss.\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Rest, drink fluids as tolerated, and wear light clothing. A light blanket can ease chills and can be removed once you feel warm. \cite{ref295}
\item[] \textbf{GEN-006.} \textbf{Myth:} Fever is always dangerous.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Fever is a common response to infection. Many fevers resolve as the illness improves. Age, symptoms, and underlying health conditions help determine how urgently someone needs care.\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Seek prompt medical assessment for a baby younger than three months with a rectal temperature of 38$^\circ$C (100.4$^\circ$F) or higher. At any age, difficulty breathing or being very hard to wake warrants emergency care. \cite{ref295}
\item[] \textbf{GEN-007.} \textbf{Myth:} Antibiotics cure colds and flu.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Colds and influenza are caused by viruses. Antibiotics treat susceptible bacteria and do not shorten either viral illness. A bacterial complication, such as bacterial pneumonia, may need separate treatment.\newline
\textbf{Implications:} If you suspect flu and are at higher risk of severe illness, contact a clinician promptly. Antiviral treatment for influenza works best when started early. \cite{ref294,ref242}
\item[] \textbf{GEN-008.} \textbf{Myth:} Stronger antibiotics are always better.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Antibiotics differ in which bacteria they can treat. A narrow-spectrum drug can be an effective first choice when the likely cause is known. A serious infection of uncertain cause may require broader initial coverage.\newline
\textbf{Implications:} If laboratory results identify the bacteria and which drugs will work, the clinician can reassess treatment. Switching to a narrower antibiotic can treat the infection while limiting unnecessary exposure to other antibiotics. \cite{ref296}
\item[] \textbf{GEN-009.} \textbf{Myth:} If symptoms improve, an infection must be completely gone.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Feeling better is a sign of recovery, but symptoms alone cannot establish that an infection has cleared. Treatment duration depends on the infection and the medicine prescribed.\newline
\textbf{Implications:} If you have been prescribed an antibiotic, follow the prescribed schedule. Contact the prescriber if symptoms return, side effects develop, or you are unsure whether to continue. \cite{ref294}
\item[] \textbf{GEN-010.} \textbf{Myth:} Natural remedies are inherently safe.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} A remedy's safety depends on its ingredients, dose, and interactions with other medicines. For example, the herb St. John's wort can reduce the effectiveness of birth control pills and of cyclosporine, a medicine used to prevent transplant rejection.\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Tell your clinician or pharmacist which herbal products you use. If you take prescription medicine, ask about interactions before starting a new remedy. \cite{ref297}
\item[] \textbf{GEN-011.} \textbf{Myth:} Cancer is always a death sentence.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} The outlook after a cancer diagnosis depends on the type, stage, and response to treatment. Some cancers can be cured, and others can be controlled for years. \cite{ref16}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Ask what the diagnosis means for your situation. Survival figures describe groups and cannot predict an individual's outcome.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-012.} \textbf{Myth:} Cancer is one disease.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Cancer is a family of diseases. Even tumors that start in the same organ can differ in how they grow and respond to treatment. \cite{ref16}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} The pathology report identifies features that help the care team choose treatment.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-013.} \textbf{Myth:} Cancer is contagious.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Ordinary contact with someone who has cancer cannot transmit the cancer. Some infections, such as HPV, can spread between people and raise the risk of certain cancers later. \cite{ref16}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} It is safe to visit, hug, and share meals with someone who has cancer.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-014.} \textbf{Myth:} A biopsy causes cancer to spread.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} A biopsy allows a pathologist to examine suspicious tissue. Tumor cells can very rarely grow along a biopsy track. The risk depends on the tumor and procedure. \cite{ref16}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Ask how the biopsy will be performed and how its result will guide treatment.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-015.} \textbf{Myth:} Surgery exposes cancer to air and makes it spread.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Exposure to air during surgery does not cause cancer to spread. Sometimes an operation reveals disease that was already present but too small to see on scans. \cite{ref16}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Ask the surgeon what the operation is intended to achieve and what the findings could change.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-016.} \textbf{Myth:} Sugar directly ``feeds'' cancer, so eliminating sugar cures it.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Healthy cells and cancer cells both use glucose. Eliminating dietary sugar has not been shown to shrink or cure cancer. \cite{ref16}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} During treatment, seek dietary advice if eating is difficult or you are losing weight unintentionally.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-017.} \textbf{Myth:} Only smokers get lung cancer.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Smoking is a major cause of lung cancer. People who have never smoked can also develop it. Other risk factors include radon and secondhand smoke. \cite{ref309}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Persistent unexplained symptoms such as coughing up blood deserve medical assessment regardless of smoking history.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-018.} \textbf{Myth:} Cancer always produces symptoms early.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Some cancers cause few or no symptoms in their early stages. The pattern varies by cancer type and location. \cite{ref16}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Follow screening recommendations that apply to your age and risk, and report new or persistent symptoms even between screenings.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-019.} \textbf{Myth:} Normal blood tests rule out cancer.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Routine blood counts and chemistry results can be normal in someone with cancer. Tests used to investigate a symptom depend on the organ involved and the clinical findings. \cite{ref310}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Follow up on a persistent unexplained symptom even if initial blood tests are normal.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-020.} \textbf{Myth:} Alternative medicine can replace proven cancer treatment.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} An observational study of patients with selected cancers found poorer survival among those who chose alternative medicine as their initial treatment in place of conventional care. The study cannot establish the effect of every individual remedy. \cite{ref243}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Discuss complementary approaches with the oncology team so that interactions and potential delays in treatment can be addressed.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-021.} \textbf{Myth:} Wearing a bra causes breast cancer.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} A study comparing postmenopausal women with and without breast cancer found no association with the bra-wearing habits examined, including underwire use and hours worn. \cite{ref244}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Choose a bra for fit and comfort. Wearing one is not an established breast cancer risk factor.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-022.} \textbf{Myth:} Wearing a loose bra causes breast cancer.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Available research on bra habits provides no evidence that a loose bra causes breast cancer. Studies of bra use cannot establish the effect of every possible fit. \cite{ref244}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Choose the fit you find comfortable. Changing to a tighter bra has no established cancer-prevention benefit.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-023.} \textbf{Myth:} Every breast lump is cancer.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Many breast lumps are benign. Cysts and fibroadenomas are two possible causes. Examination and imaging, and sometimes a biopsy, help determine the cause. \cite{ref311}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Arrange assessment of a new breast lump, even if a previous lump was benign.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-024.} \textbf{Myth:} Breast cancer lumps are always painful.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} A breast cancer lump can be painless or tender. Pain alone cannot identify the cause of a lump. \cite{ref311}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Tell a clinician about a new lump whether or not it hurts.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-025.} \textbf{Myth:} A painless breast lump is harmless.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Both benign lumps and breast cancers can be painless. Waiting for a lump to hurt can delay diagnosis. \cite{ref311}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Arrange assessment when you notice a new lump.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-026.} \textbf{Myth:} Men cannot get breast cancer.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Men have breast tissue and can develop breast cancer, although it is much less common in men than in women. \cite{ref245}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} A new breast lump, nipple retraction, or bloody nipple discharge in a man warrants assessment.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-027.} \textbf{Myth:} Mammography gives enough radiation to make screening more dangerous than useful.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Mammography uses a small dose of ionizing radiation. For groups advised to undergo screening, the expected benefit of earlier detection outweighs the small radiation risk. False alarms and overdiagnosis are also relevant harms. \cite{ref246}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Discuss a screening schedule appropriate to your age and risk.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-028.} \textbf{Myth:} Ultrasound can completely replace mammography.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Ultrasound can show whether a lump is fluid-filled or solid. Mammography can reveal tiny calcium deposits that may be difficult to detect on ultrasound. \cite{ref312}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} The appropriate first test for a lump can differ from the test used for routine screening. Follow the imaging plan recommended for your situation.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-029.} \textbf{Myth:} BI-RADS 5 is a definitive cancer diagnosis.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} BI-RADS 5 means the imaging finding is highly suggestive of cancer, with an estimated likelihood of at least 95 percent. A tissue diagnosis usually follows. \cite{ref274}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Arrange the recommended biopsy promptly. Its findings guide the next steps.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-030.} \textbf{Myth:} Heart disease affects mostly men.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Heart disease affects women as well as men and is a major cause of death in both. Risk varies with age, blood pressure, smoking, diabetes, and other factors. \cite{ref46}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Consider your own risk factors when discussing prevention.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-031.} \textbf{Myth:} Heart attacks always cause dramatic chest pain.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Chest discomfort is common during a heart attack, but it may be mild or absent. Shortness of breath, nausea, sweating, or discomfort in the arm or jaw can also occur. \cite{ref46}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Call local emergency services for a suspected heart attack, even if you are unsure or symptoms ease.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-032.} \textbf{Myth:} Young people cannot have heart attacks.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Heart attacks become more common with age, but young adults can have them. Causes include coronary artery disease and, less commonly, a tear in a coronary artery wall. \cite{ref46,ref247}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Seek emergency help for symptoms suggesting a heart attack at any age.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-033.} \textbf{Myth:} High blood pressure always causes symptoms.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} High blood pressure commonly causes no noticeable symptoms. The only way to know the pressure is to measure it. \cite{ref272}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Have blood pressure checked even when you feel well.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-034.} \textbf{Myth:} If you feel fine, your blood pressure must be normal.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} How you feel is an unreliable guide to blood pressure. Stress and activity can affect a reading, and high readings can occur without discomfort. \cite{ref272}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Use properly taken measurements and discuss repeated high readings with a clinician.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-035.} \textbf{Myth:} Once blood pressure becomes normal on medication, hypertension is cured.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} A normal reading while taking blood pressure medicine often means the treatment is working. Pressure can rise again if treatment stops. \cite{ref272}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Discuss repeated low readings or side effects with the prescriber before changing the dose.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-036.} \textbf{Myth:} All dietary cholesterol directly becomes blood cholesterol.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} The body makes cholesterol and regulates how much it absorbs from food. Blood cholesterol reflects several influences, including genetics and the types of fat in the diet. \cite{ref10}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Look at the overall eating pattern when deciding how to lower LDL cholesterol.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-037.} \textbf{Myth:} All fats are bad for the heart.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Different fats affect blood cholesterol differently. Replacing some saturated fat with unsaturated fat can lower LDL cholesterol. \cite{ref10}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} For example, use an unsaturated vegetable oil in place of butter for some meals.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-038.} \textbf{Myth:} A normal ECG proves the heart is completely healthy.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} A resting ECG records the heart's electrical activity for a short time. It can miss intermittent rhythm problems, and a normal tracing alone cannot rule out a heart attack. \cite{ref46}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Seek urgent care for suspected heart attack symptoms even if an earlier ECG was normal.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-039.} \textbf{Myth:} Coughing vigorously can reliably stop your own heart attack.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Coughing cannot reliably treat a heart attack or restore circulation during cardiac arrest. \cite{ref299}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Call local emergency services for a suspected heart attack. If someone collapses and is unresponsive and not breathing normally, start CPR and use an available AED as directed.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-040.} \textbf{Myth:} Eating sugar directly causes diabetes.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Type 1 diabetes usually results from autoimmune destruction of insulin-producing cells. Type 2 diabetes has several causes. Regular consumption of sugary drinks is associated with greater type 2 diabetes risk. \cite{ref173,ref224}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Replacing sugary drinks with water is one practical way to reduce added sugar intake.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-041.} \textbf{Myth:} People with diabetes can never eat carbohydrates.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} People with diabetes can eat carbohydrate-containing foods, including beans, fruit, and whole grains. Amount, food choice, and medication affect the blood glucose response. \cite{ref173}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Plan portions around your glucose targets and treatment regimen.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-042.} \textbf{Myth:} Diabetes is always caused by obesity.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} People at many body weights develop diabetes. Higher body fat is one risk factor for type 2 diabetes. Type 1 diabetes generally has an autoimmune cause. \cite{ref173,ref224}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Weight alone cannot identify the type or cause of someone's diabetes.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-043.} \textbf{Myth:} Thin people cannot develop type 2 diabetes.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Type 2 diabetes can develop in people with a lean build. Family history, age, and other factors also influence risk. \cite{ref173,ref224}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Discuss testing if you have symptoms or risk factors, whatever your weight.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-044.} \textbf{Myth:} Diabetes always causes obvious symptoms.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Type 2 diabetes can develop gradually with few noticeable symptoms. When symptoms occur, they may include increased thirst and frequent urination. \cite{ref173}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Do not wait for symptoms if screening is recommended for you.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-045.} \textbf{Myth:} One normal fasting glucose proves you do not have diabetes.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Fasting glucose measures blood sugar at one point in time. A person with a normal fasting value may meet diagnostic criteria on another test, such as an oral glucose tolerance test. \cite{ref300}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} If symptoms or risk factors persist, ask whether repeat or different testing is appropriate.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-046.} \textbf{Myth:} Honey is harmless for blood glucose because it is natural.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Honey contains sugars that raise blood glucose. The effect depends partly on the amount eaten. \cite{ref173}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Include honey when counting carbohydrate or planning a meal.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-047.} \textbf{Myth:} Brown sugar is substantially healthier for diabetes than white sugar.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Brown and white table sugar contain similar amounts of carbohydrate by weight. The small amount of molasses in brown sugar gives it color and flavor. \cite{ref173}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Treat both as added sugars when planning portions.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-048.} \textbf{Myth:} Starting insulin means you have ``failed.''\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Insulin replaces or supplements insulin the body cannot supply in adequate amounts. It is essential in type 1 diabetes and can be needed temporarily or long term in type 2 diabetes. \cite{ref173,ref224}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Ask how to use insulin safely and recognize low blood sugar. Needing treatment is no measure of personal effort.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-049.} \textbf{Myth:} Diabetes medicines make the pancreas permanently dependent on them.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Diabetes medicines work in different ways. If blood sugar rises after a medicine is stopped, the underlying diabetes may still require treatment. \cite{ref173,ref224}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Review treatment with the prescriber before stopping a medicine that has helped you reach your glucose targets.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-050.} \textbf{Myth:} Humans use only 10 percent of their brains.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Brain imaging shows that different regions contribute to different tasks and that many networks remain active at rest. There is no fixed reserve of 90 percent that normally lies unused. \cite{ref30}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Claims that a product unlocks unused brain capacity rest on a false premise.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-051.} \textbf{Myth:} Adults cannot form new brain cells.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Evidence from cell dating and studies of human tissue supports formation of some new neurons in the adult hippocampus. Estimates of how many form and how this changes with age vary by method. \cite{ref248,ref301}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Adult learning also involves changes in connections among existing neurons. Evidence for new neurons in one region does not establish widespread brain regeneration.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-052.} \textbf{Myth:} Memory works like a perfect video recording.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Remembering involves reconstructing an event from stored details, context, and later information. A memory can be vivid and sincerely held while some details are wrong. \cite{ref30}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} For a detail that matters, check a contemporaneous record when one is available.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-053.} \textbf{Myth:} Mental illness is simply a lack of willpower.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Mental disorders arise from interacting biological, psychological, and social factors. Symptoms themselves can affect motivation and the ability to carry out everyday tasks. \cite{ref190}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Offer practical support and encourage assessment when symptoms interfere with daily life.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-054.} \textbf{Myth:} Depression is just ordinary sadness.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Depression can involve persistent loss of interest as well as low mood, with changes in sleep, energy, or concentration. Clinicians assess the pattern, duration, and impact on daily life. \cite{ref63}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Seek help when symptoms persist or interfere with activities. Severe distress deserves attention promptly.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-055.} \textbf{Myth:} People with depression can simply ``snap out of it.''\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Depression can impair energy, concentration, and initiative. Recovery may involve psychotherapy, medication, social support, or a combination. \cite{ref63}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} A concrete offer, such as helping arrange an appointment, can be easier to act on than an instruction to think positively.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-056.} \textbf{Myth:} Dementia is an inevitable part of normal aging.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Dementia involves cognitive decline that interferes with daily functioning. Risk rises with age, but many older adults never develop dementia. \cite{ref249}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Seek assessment for changes that disrupt familiar tasks. There are several possible causes.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-057.} \textbf{Myth:} Alzheimer's disease and dementia are synonymous.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Dementia describes a syndrome. Alzheimer's disease is one cause. Others include vascular disease and Lewy body disease, and causes can coexist. \cite{ref189}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} A clinical assessment can help clarify the cause and guide care.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-058.} \textbf{Myth:} All seizures involve violent shaking.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Some seizures involve staring, brief loss of awareness, unusual sensations, or repetitive movements. Convulsive shaking is only one possible presentation. \cite{ref71}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} If you witness an episode, note its duration and what you observed. These details can help the clinician assessing it.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-059.} \textbf{Myth:} You should put something in the mouth of someone having a seizure.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} A person cannot swallow their tongue during a seizure. An object placed between the teeth can injure the mouth or obstruct breathing. \cite{ref302}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Clear nearby hazards, cushion the head, turn the person gently onto their side when possible, and time the seizure. Call emergency services for a first seizure, one lasting over five minutes, or difficulty breathing afterward.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-060.} \textbf{Myth:} Everyone needs a daily detox.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} The liver processes many substances, and the kidneys excrete many waste products. Evidence does not support commercial daily cleanses for healthy people. \cite{ref303}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} A suspected poisoning needs substance-specific medical advice. Contact a poison service or emergency service promptly.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-061.} \textbf{Myth:} Detox juices remove unspecified toxins from the body.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Evidence for a detox claim requires an identified substance and evidence that the intervention removes it safely. Studies have not established that juice cleanses deliver the advertised benefits. \cite{ref303}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Choose juice as a food if you enjoy it. A juice-only regimen can displace foods that supply protein and fiber.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-062.} \textbf{Myth:} Gluten is unhealthy for everyone.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Most people can eat foods containing gluten. People with celiac disease need a gluten-free diet, and some people have other conditions requiring specific dietary advice. \cite{ref194}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} If celiac disease is suspected, discuss testing before removing gluten because avoidance can affect test results.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-063.} \textbf{Myth:} Carbohydrates are inherently unhealthy.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Carbohydrate-containing foods vary widely. Beans, whole grains, and fruit provide fiber and nutrients, while sugary drinks supply added sugar with little fiber. \cite{ref191}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Choose carbohydrate sources and portions that suit your health and dietary needs.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-064.} \textbf{Myth:} Eating fat automatically makes you fat.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Fat contributes energy to meals, but eating a food that contains fat does not by itself determine whether body fat increases. Weight changes reflect intake and expenditure over time. \cite{ref191}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Consider portion sizes and the overall eating pattern. Unsaturated fats can be part of a balanced diet.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-065.} \textbf{Myth:} Skipping breakfast damages everyone's metabolism.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Evidence does not establish that everyone needs breakfast to maintain a healthy metabolism. Whether breakfast helps depends in part on appetite, schedule, and what is eaten later. \cite{ref191}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Choose a workable meal pattern. If medication needs to be taken with food, account for that when planning meals.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-066.} \textbf{Myth:} Eating after a particular hour automatically causes weight gain.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Eating after a particular hour does not guarantee weight gain. Meal timing can affect hunger and eating habits, while long-term energy balance also matters. \cite{ref191}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} If late snacks add more food than you intend to eat, adjusting their size or timing may help.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-067.} \textbf{Myth:} Sea salt is dramatically healthier than table salt.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Sea salt and table salt contain similar amounts of sodium by weight. Coarse and fine crystals can pack differently into a teaspoon. Iodized salt also provides iodine. \cite{ref46}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} To reduce sodium intake, pay attention to the amount used and the sodium in prepared foods.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-068.} \textbf{Myth:} Supplements can replace a balanced diet.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Supplements can provide a specific nutrient when intake is inadequate or needs are increased. A multivitamin cannot supply all the protein, fiber, or other components of a varied diet. \cite{ref20}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Use supplements for an identified purpose and continue to address dietary needs.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-069.} \textbf{Myth:} More vitamins are always better.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} The benefit and risk of a vitamin depend on the dose and the person's needs. Excessive intake of certain vitamins can cause harm, particularly when several products contain the same ingredient. \cite{ref20}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Check the amounts on supplement labels before combining products.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-070.} \textbf{Myth:} Antibiotics prevent every infection.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Antibiotics can prevent certain bacterial infections in defined settings, such as selected operations. They do not prevent every infection or protect against ordinary viral colds. \cite{ref294}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Use preventive antibiotics only for the indication and duration prescribed.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-071.} \textbf{Myth:} Vaccines cause the disease they are designed to prevent.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Many vaccines contain no live organism capable of causing the target infection. Some live attenuated vaccines can rarely cause vaccine-associated illness, particularly in people with certain immune conditions. \cite{ref314}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Ask which vaccine is appropriate if your immune system is weakened. Brief soreness or fever after a dose is a common immune response.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-072.} \textbf{Myth:} Vaccines cause autism.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Large studies have found no increased autism risk after MMR vaccination. The timing of early childhood vaccination overlaps with the age when developmental differences often become noticeable. \cite{ref1,ref2}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Raise developmental concerns with a clinician so they can be assessed promptly.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-073.} \textbf{Myth:} Vaccination weakens the immune system.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Vaccines stimulate immune memory against particular infections. The recommended vaccination schedule has not been shown to weaken the immune system. \cite{ref314}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} People with immune conditions may need different vaccine choices or timing. Ask about the schedule appropriate to your circumstances.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-074.} \textbf{Myth:} Natural infection is always safer than vaccination.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Infection can produce immunity, but acquiring it entails the risks of the disease and its complications. Vaccines can provide protection with lower risk for the people for whom they are recommended. \cite{ref314}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Use the recommendations for the particular vaccine, age, and health condition when weighing benefits and risks.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-075.} \textbf{Myth:} Once vaccinated, infection becomes impossible.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Vaccine protection varies by vaccine, time since vaccination, and the person receiving it. Vaccinated people can sometimes become infected and may still have substantial protection against severe illness. \cite{ref314}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Follow guidance on additional doses and seek assessment for concerning symptoms regardless of vaccination history.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-076.} \textbf{Myth:} Hand sanitizer is always superior to soap and water.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Soap and water remove dirt and are preferred when hands are visibly soiled. An alcohol-based sanitizer containing at least 60 percent alcohol is a useful option when washing facilities are unavailable. \cite{ref315}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Wash with soap and water after using the toilet and before preparing food.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-077.} \textbf{Myth:} You can tell whether an infection is viral or bacterial solely from fever severity.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Both bacterial and viral infections can cause high fever. Some serious infections cause little fever, especially in people with impaired immune responses. \cite{ref295}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Decisions about evaluation and antibiotics depend on the whole illness, including symptoms, examination, and sometimes testing.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-078.} \textbf{Myth:} Antibiotics should be taken whenever you have a sore throat.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Most sore throats are viral. Group A streptococcal throat infection is one cause for which testing and antibiotic treatment may be appropriate. \cite{ref316}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Seek assessment for severe or persistent symptoms. Difficulty breathing warrants emergency care.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-079.} \textbf{Myth:} Antibiotic resistance means your body has become resistant to antibiotics.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Antibiotic resistance develops in bacteria. Resistant bacteria can survive treatment with a drug that would usually kill them or stop their growth. \cite{ref3}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Taking antibiotics only when indicated helps limit the selection and spread of resistant bacteria.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-080.} \textbf{Myth:} Cracking your knuckles causes arthritis.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Observational studies have not found a clear association between habitual knuckle cracking and hand osteoarthritis. These studies cannot exclude every possible joint injury. \cite{ref304}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Have persistent pain, swelling, or restricted movement assessed, regardless of whether a joint cracks.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-081.} \textbf{Myth:} Exercise is bad for arthritic joints.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Appropriately chosen exercise can reduce osteoarthritis pain and improve strength and function. The best activity and intensity depend on the joint and the person's symptoms. \cite{ref317}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Start at a manageable level and adjust the plan if an activity repeatedly increases pain or swelling.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-082.} \textbf{Myth:} Back pain always means a slipped disc.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} A herniated disc is one possible cause of back pain. Many episodes have no single identifiable structural cause, and disc changes can also appear on scans of people without pain. \cite{ref250}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} New bladder or bowel dysfunction, numbness around the groin, or progressive leg weakness with back pain requires urgent assessment.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-083.} \textbf{Myth:} Bed rest is the best treatment for ordinary low-back pain.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} For uncomplicated low-back pain, continuing tolerable everyday activity usually supports recovery. Prolonged bed rest can increase stiffness and reduce conditioning. \cite{ref250}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Briefly modify activities that aggravate symptoms and return to them gradually as tolerated.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-084.} \textbf{Myth:} Weight training is only for young people.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Older adults can gain strength and improve their ability to perform everyday tasks through resistance exercise. Exercises can use body weight, bands, or weights. \cite{ref318}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Choose an initial load you can control and increase it gradually. Seek individualized guidance if balance or a medical condition affects safe participation.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-085.} \textbf{Myth:} Running inevitably destroys your knees.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Recreational running has not been consistently associated with increased knee osteoarthritis. Observational comparisons cannot prove that running protects every knee. Prior injury and training history matter. \cite{ref251}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Build mileage gradually and seek assessment for persistent pain or swelling.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-086.} \textbf{Myth:} Muscle soreness proves a workout was effective.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Unfamiliar exercise can cause delayed soreness. As the body adapts, the same activity may cause little soreness even while fitness improves. \cite{ref193}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Track progress through measures such as strength, endurance, and ability to repeat a task comfortably.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-087.} \textbf{Myth:} You can selectively burn abdominal fat by doing sit-ups.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Sit-ups strengthen abdominal muscles. They do not reliably produce a selective reduction in the fat covering those muscles. \cite{ref305}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Choose abdominal exercises for strength and function. Changes in body fat depend on a wider pattern of activity and energy intake.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-088.} \textbf{Myth:} Sweating more means you are burning substantially more fat.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Sweating helps cool the body. Heat, humidity, clothing, and individual differences can change sweat loss even at the same workload. \cite{ref191}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} A lower weight immediately after a sweaty session largely reflects lost fluid. Sweat volume is an unreliable measure of fat loss.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-089.} \textbf{Myth:} No pain, no gain.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Training can feel demanding without being painful. Sharp, worsening, or persistent pain can signal that an activity needs to be stopped or modified. \cite{ref193}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Build effort progressively and seek advice for pain that persists or affects normal movement.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-090.} \textbf{Myth:} Reading in dim light permanently damages your eyes.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Dim lighting can make reading more tiring, but ordinary reading in dim light has not been shown to permanently damage the eyes. \cite{ref306}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Use enough light to read comfortably. Persistent difficulty seeing in low light deserves an eye examination.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-091.} \textbf{Myth:} Sitting close to a television permanently damages your eyes.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Sitting close to a television can cause temporary discomfort, but ordinary viewing at close range has not been shown to cause permanent eye damage. \cite{ref306}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} If a child repeatedly moves close to see details, consider a vision check for an existing focusing problem.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-092.} \textbf{Myth:} Wearing glasses makes your eyes progressively weaker.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Ordinary prescription glasses correct how light focuses while they are worn. A prescription may change as the eyes grow or age. \cite{ref306}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Use the prescribed correction and arrange a review if vision remains blurred while wearing it.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-093.} \textbf{Myth:} Cataracts can be permanently removed with eye drops.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} A cataract is clouding of the eye's natural lens. No established eye drop removes it. Surgery replaces the cloudy lens when its effect on vision warrants treatment. \cite{ref175}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Ask how much the cataract affects activities such as reading or driving when deciding on surgery.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-094.} \textbf{Myth:} Acne is simply caused by dirty skin.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Acne develops when follicles become plugged and inflamed. Oil production and hormones contribute. Scrubbing aggressively can further irritate the skin. \cite{ref313}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Wash gently and seek treatment if acne persists, scars, or causes distress.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-095.} \textbf{Myth:} Toothpaste is a good treatment for acne.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Toothpaste is formulated for teeth, and its ingredients can irritate inflamed skin. It is not an established acne treatment. \cite{ref313}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Choose a product formulated for acne or ask a pharmacist or clinician which treatment suits your skin.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-096.} \textbf{Myth:} A base tan protects you from harmful ultraviolet radiation.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} A tan indicates that the skin has responded to ultraviolet exposure. Any protection it provides is too limited to prevent further UV injury reliably. \cite{ref319}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Use shade, protective clothing, and sunscreen instead of trying to develop a protective tan.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-097.} \textbf{Myth:} Sunscreen is needed only on hot, sunny days.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} UV exposure depends on more than air temperature. Substantial UV can reach the skin through clouds and on cool days. \cite{ref319}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Consider the UV index and time outdoors. Use shade, clothing, and broad-spectrum sunscreen on exposed skin when protection is needed.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-098.} \textbf{Myth:} Wounds heal faster when routinely left open to breathe.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Minor clean cuts and scrapes generally heal well when kept slightly moist and covered. A dressing also protects the wound from friction and contamination. \cite{ref307}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Clean the wound, apply a thin layer of plain petroleum jelly, and cover it with a clean dressing. Seek care for deep wounds, bites, or signs of infection.
\item[] \textbf{GEN-099.} \textbf{Myth:} Hydrogen peroxide or alcohol should routinely be poured into every wound.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Hydrogen peroxide and rubbing alcohol can irritate exposed tissue. Clean running water is appropriate for rinsing an ordinary minor cut. \cite{ref320}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Rinse away debris and cover the wound. Seek care for an embedded object, a deep wound, or bleeding that persists despite direct pressure.

\section{Reproductive, Pediatric, Respiratory, Dermatologic, and Aging Myths}

The following entries extend the registry with additional reproductive, pediatric, respiratory, dermatologic, aging, and treatment myths. The supporting source set includes women's-health and general medical myth guidance; individual claims should still be checked against current local recommendations \cite{ref13,ref11,ref12,ref14}.
\item[] \textbf{REP-001.} \textbf{Myth:} Everyone should routinely undergo huge panels of blood tests.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Screening blood tests can be valuable when selected for a person's age and risk. Large panels in people without symptoms or a specific indication can yield incidental abnormalities and lead to unnecessary follow-up. \cite{ref308}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Ask which condition each proposed test screens for and how the result would change your care.
\item[] \textbf{REP-002.} \textbf{Myth:} The vagina needs internal washing or douching.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Normal vaginal secretions carry away shed cells and fluid. Douching can disturb the usual balance of vaginal bacteria. \cite{ref321}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Wash the vulva gently. A new or persistent odor warrants assessment rather than an internal wash.
\item[] \textbf{REP-003.} \textbf{Myth:} Vaginal discharge always indicates infection.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Clear or white discharge without a strong odor is common and can change during the menstrual cycle. Discharge alone cannot diagnose an infection. \cite{ref322}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Seek advice if the discharge changes markedly or comes with itching, pain, bleeding, or a strong odor.
\item[] \textbf{REP-004.} \textbf{Myth:} Pregnancy cannot occur during menstruation.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Pregnancy can result from intercourse during menstrual bleeding. Sperm can survive for several days, so intercourse near the end of a period can precede an early ovulation. \cite{ref253}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Bleeding alone does not identify a day when unprotected intercourse is free of pregnancy risk.
\item[] \textbf{REP-005.} \textbf{Myth:} Pregnancy cannot occur the first time someone has intercourse.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Pregnancy is possible the first time someone has vaginal intercourse if sperm reach an egg. The chance depends on timing and contraception, just as it does on later occasions. \cite{ref253,ref323}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} If pregnancy is unwanted after unprotected intercourse, seek timely advice about emergency contraception.
\item[] \textbf{REP-006.} \textbf{Myth:} Oral contraceptives permanently cause infertility.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Oral contraceptives temporarily prevent pregnancy. Fertility generally returns promptly after stopping them, and ovulation can occur before the next period. \cite{ref323}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} If pregnancy is unwanted, arrange another contraceptive method when stopping the pill.
\item[] \textbf{REP-007.} \textbf{Myth:} Menopause is a disease.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Natural menopause is a stage of life recognized after twelve consecutive months without menstruation when there is no other cause. Symptoms vary considerably. \cite{ref324}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Seek advice for troublesome symptoms. Bleeding after menopause also warrants assessment.
\item[] \textbf{REP-008.} \textbf{Myth:} Cervical cancer is hereditary in the same way as many inherited cancer syndromes.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Persistent infection with a high-risk type of human papillomavirus (HPV) causes nearly all cervical cancers. Most HPV infections clear without causing cancer. \cite{ref325}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Follow local HPV vaccination and cervical screening recommendations even without a family history of cervical cancer.
\item[] \textbf{REP-009.} \textbf{Myth:} Pregnant women should literally ``eat for two.''\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Pregnancy increases nutrient needs, but it does not double energy needs. In a typical singleton pregnancy, extra energy requirements are modest and generally increase later in pregnancy. \cite{ref326}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Choose varied, nutrient-rich foods. A clinician can tailor advice for multiple pregnancy, weight changes, or difficulty eating.
\item[] \textbf{REP-010.} \textbf{Myth:} Exercise is inherently dangerous during pregnancy.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} For most people with uncomplicated pregnancies, regular moderate physical activity is safe and beneficial. Certain pregnancy complications require changes to the type or amount of activity. \cite{ref254}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Discuss activity restrictions with the maternity team if complications arise. Stop exercising and seek advice for warning symptoms such as vaginal bleeding.
\item[] \textbf{REP-011.} \textbf{Myth:} Heartburn during pregnancy reliably predicts a baby with lots of hair.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} In a study of 64 pregnancies, greater reported heartburn was associated with more newborn hair. That study did not establish a reliable way to predict an individual baby's hair or show that fetal hair causes reflux. \cite{ref255}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Treat troublesome heartburn on its own merits. A prediction based on this small study carries considerable uncertainty.
\item[] \textbf{REP-012.} \textbf{Myth:} The shape of the abdomen predicts the baby's sex.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} The appearance of a pregnant abdomen varies with fetal position, stage of pregnancy, and the pregnant person's build. Its shape is not a validated test of fetal sex. \cite{ref327}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} A change that concerns you should be discussed with the maternity team on its own merits.
\item[] \textbf{REP-013.} \textbf{Myth:} Fetal heart rate reliably predicts fetal sex.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} A systematic review found that first-trimester fetal heart rate did not reliably distinguish male from female fetuses. The proposed fast-heartbeat and slow-heartbeat cutoffs are unreliable. \cite{ref328}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Clinicians interpret fetal heart rate in the context of gestational age and the baby's condition.
\item[] \textbf{REP-014.} \textbf{Myth:} Breastfeeding completely prevents pregnancy.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Breastfeeding can delay ovulation. The lactational amenorrhea method is effective when all three conditions hold: the baby is under six months old, periods have not returned, and breastfeeding is full or nearly full, with feeding intervals no longer than four hours by day or six hours at night. \cite{ref329}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Use another contraceptive method as soon as any condition no longer holds if pregnancy is unwanted. Ovulation can precede the first period.
\item[] \textbf{REP-015.} \textbf{Myth:} Infant formula and breast milk are biologically identical.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Human milk contains antibodies and other components that change over time. Commercial infant formula is designed to meet infants' nutritional needs and has a different composition. \cite{ref68,ref330}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Families using formula should follow preparation and storage instructions for the specific product.
\item[] \textbf{REP-016.} \textbf{Myth:} Babies need additional water during their first six months.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} For most healthy infants under six months, breast milk or correctly prepared infant formula supplies the water they need. Extra water can displace feeds, and excessive water can dangerously lower blood sodium. \cite{ref330,ref256}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Mix formula exactly as directed. If an infant is feeding poorly or seems dehydrated, seek medical advice before offering extra water.
\item[] \textbf{REP-017.} \textbf{Myth:} Teething routinely causes high fever.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Teething can cause tender gums, drooling, and a desire to chew. A high fever needs another explanation. \cite{ref331,ref295}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Check the temperature of a baby who feels hot. A rectal temperature of 38 degrees Celsius or higher in an infant younger than three months needs prompt medical assessment.
\item[] \textbf{REP-018.} \textbf{Myth:} Picking up a crying newborn will inevitably spoil the baby.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Newborns cry to signal needs such as hunger, discomfort, or the need for closeness. Responding and holding them is part of responsive care. \cite{ref70}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Check feeding and comfort needs. If crying becomes overwhelming, place the baby on their back in a safe crib and take a short break. Never shake a baby.
\item[] \textbf{REP-019.} \textbf{Myth:} Children are simply miniature adults medically.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Children's bodies change how they absorb, process, and clear medicines as they grow. A suitable dose can depend on age, weight, formulation, and the medicine itself. \cite{ref257}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Use pediatric instructions for the exact product. Ask a clinician or pharmacist before adapting an adult dose.
\item[] \textbf{REP-020.} \textbf{Myth:} A chubby child is necessarily a healthy child.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Appearance alone cannot establish a child's nutritional status or overall health. Clinicians assess growth over time alongside development, medical history, and examination. \cite{ref258}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Discuss concerns using the child's growth record and avoid assigning health or blame on the basis of body shape.
\item[] \textbf{REP-021.} \textbf{Myth:} Antibiotics help children recover from every fever faster.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Fever can accompany viral or bacterial illness. Antibiotics can help certain bacterial infections and do not shorten a viral fever. \cite{ref294,ref295}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Seek assessment based on the child's age, symptoms, and condition. Use antibiotics only when prescribed for the illness.
\item[] \textbf{REP-022.} \textbf{Myth:} Children cannot have high blood pressure.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Children can have high blood pressure, often without symptoms. Interpretation depends on age and, for younger children, sex and height. Correct cuff size matters. \cite{ref259}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} An elevated reading usually needs confirmation with carefully repeated measurements. Discuss follow-up with the child's clinician.
\item[] \textbf{REP-023.} \textbf{Myth:} Children cannot develop type 2 diabetes.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Type 2 diabetes can develop in childhood, especially around or after puberty. Type 1 diabetes also occurs in children and needs different management. \cite{ref260}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} New thirst, frequent urination, or unexplained weight loss warrants prompt assessment. Symptoms alone cannot distinguish the two types.
\item[] \textbf{REP-024.} \textbf{Myth:} Vaccines overload a child's immune system.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Recommended childhood vaccines can be given together. Evidence supports the safety of the combinations used in routine schedules, and separating doses can leave a child susceptible for longer. \cite{ref332}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Follow the local schedule and discuss any previous serious reaction or immune condition with the child's clinician.
\item[] \textbf{REP-025.} \textbf{Myth:} Fever always causes brain damage in children.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Most fevers from infection resolve without brain injury. The underlying illness can still be serious. Heatstroke is a different condition in which uncontrolled overheating can damage organs. \cite{ref8,ref295}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Consider age, alertness, breathing, and fluid intake. An infant under three months with a rectal temperature of 38 degrees Celsius or higher needs prompt assessment.
\item[] \textbf{REP-026.} \textbf{Myth:} Every febrile seizure means epilepsy.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} A febrile seizure is a seizure associated with fever in a young child. A single simple febrile seizure does not establish epilepsy, and most affected children do not develop epilepsy. \cite{ref333,ref302}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Protect the child from injury, time the seizure, and keep the mouth clear of objects. Call emergency services for a first seizure, one lasting over five minutes, or breathing difficulty afterward.
\item[] \textbf{REP-027.} \textbf{Myth:} Aspirin is an ordinary fever medicine for children.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Aspirin use during some viral illnesses in children and teenagers is associated with Reye syndrome, a rare but serious illness affecting the liver and brain. Specialists prescribe aspirin for certain conditions. \cite{ref334}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Do not give aspirin to a child for fever unless specifically directed by a clinician. Ask which medicine and dose are appropriate for the child's age and weight.
\item[] \textbf{REP-028.} \textbf{Myth:} Asthma is primarily a psychological disease.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Asthma affects the airways, causing variable narrowing and inflammation. Stress or strong emotion can trigger symptoms in some people, as can exercise, allergens, and respiratory infections. \cite{ref179}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Follow the asthma action plan when symptoms occur, including when stress is a trigger.
\item[] \textbf{REP-029.} \textbf{Myth:} People with asthma should not exercise.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} People with well-controlled asthma can generally exercise. Exercise can trigger airway narrowing, especially when asthma is poorly controlled or the air is cold and dry. \cite{ref179}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Use the exercise instructions in your asthma action plan. Repeated activity-limiting symptoms warrant a treatment review.
\item[] \textbf{REP-030.} \textbf{Myth:} Asthma inhalers are addictive.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Prescribed asthma inhalers do not cause addiction. Symptoms returning after a controller is stopped can mean the underlying airway inflammation still needs treatment. \cite{ref179}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} If you need your reliever more often than your action plan anticipates, ask for a review of asthma control and inhaler technique.
\item[] \textbf{REP-031.} \textbf{Myth:} Using an inhaler means your asthma has become severe.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} An inhaler is a way of delivering medicine to the airways. People across the range of asthma severity may use one. The device alone says little about severity. \cite{ref179}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Ask what each prescribed inhaler does and when to use it. Follow the written action plan if symptoms worsen.
\item[] \textbf{REP-032.} \textbf{Myth:} Tuberculosis is always obvious from symptoms.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Inactive tuberculosis infection causes no symptoms and does not spread to others. Active TB disease may start gradually, and symptoms alone cannot identify everyone who needs testing. \cite{ref177}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} If a public health team identifies you as a close contact of someone with infectious TB, follow its testing advice even if you feel well.
\item[] \textbf{REP-033.} \textbf{Myth:} Pneumonia occurs only in cold weather.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Pneumonia is an infection of the lungs that can occur in any season. Seasonal changes in circulating respiratory viruses can affect how often it occurs. \cite{ref261}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Seek assessment for concerning cough, fever, or breathlessness regardless of the weather. Severe breathing difficulty warrants emergency care.
\item[] \textbf{REP-034.} \textbf{Myth:} All pneumonia is bacterial.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Pneumonia can result from bacteria, viruses, and sometimes fungi. Symptoms overlap, and the cause may remain uncertain at first. \cite{ref261,ref262}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Treatment depends on the likely cause and severity. Do not use leftover antibiotics to treat a suspected pneumonia.
\item[] \textbf{REP-035.} \textbf{Myth:} Smoking only damages the lungs.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Smoking damages blood vessels and increases risks of heart disease, stroke, and cancers in several organs as well as the lungs. \cite{ref335}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Quitting reduces health risks even after years of smoking. A clinician can advise on cessation support.
\item[] \textbf{REP-036.} \textbf{Myth:} Occasional smoking is harmless.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} In population studies, people who smoked on some days had a higher risk of premature death than people who never smoked. Smoking fewer cigarettes reduces exposure but leaves measurable risk. \cite{ref336}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} If you smoke occasionally, cessation support is still appropriate.
\item[] \textbf{REP-037.} \textbf{Myth:} Hookah is safer than cigarettes because smoke passes through water.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} The water in a hookah does not remove enough toxic substances to make the smoke safe. Users can inhale nicotine and carbon monoxide, including carbon monoxide from burning charcoal. \cite{ref337}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} A smoother or cooler sensation is an unreliable guide to smoke exposure. People nearby can also inhale secondhand smoke.
\item[] \textbf{REP-038.} \textbf{Myth:} Shaving makes hair grow back thicker.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Shaving cuts the hair shaft at the skin surface. The blunt tip can feel coarse as it grows out, even though shaving has not increased the hair's diameter or growth rate. \cite{ref338}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Base shaving decisions on preference and skin comfort. Persistent changes in hair growth have a different cause.
\item[] \textbf{REP-039.} \textbf{Myth:} Cutting hair makes it grow faster.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Hair grows from follicles beneath the skin. Trimming the ends does not speed growth at the roots. Removing split ends can improve appearance and limit breakage. \cite{ref338}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Choose trims for hair condition and style. They are not a treatment for thinning at the scalp.
\item[] \textbf{REP-040.} \textbf{Myth:} Dandruff necessarily means poor hygiene.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Dandruff is scalp flaking with several contributing factors, including susceptibility to seborrheic dermatitis. Its presence alone tells you little about a person's hygiene. \cite{ref263}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} An antidandruff shampoo may help. Seek advice for persistent flaking, pain, or marked inflammation.
\item[] \textbf{REP-041.} \textbf{Myth:} Hair loss always comes from stress.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Stress or illness can trigger temporary shedding. Other causes include inherited pattern hair loss, autoimmune disease, and some medicines. The pattern and timing help distinguish them. \cite{ref264}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Seek assessment for sudden, patchy, persistent, or painful hair loss rather than assuming stress explains it.
\item[] \textbf{REP-042.} \textbf{Myth:} Acne should be aggressively scrubbed away.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Acne develops in hair follicles. Scrubbing can irritate inflamed skin and worsen symptoms. \cite{ref313}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Wash gently with fingertips and a mild cleanser. Persistent acne or scarring may need targeted treatment.
\item[] \textbf{REP-043.} \textbf{Myth:} Chocolate invariably causes acne.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Studies of chocolate and acne are limited, and they do not show that everyone who eats chocolate develops acne. Chocolate products also vary in sugar and milk content. \cite{ref265}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} If you notice a repeatable pattern, discuss it alongside established acne treatments before adopting a restrictive diet.
\item[] \textbf{REP-044.} \textbf{Myth:} A wound itching means it is infected.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Itching can occur as a wound heals or when a dressing irritates the skin. Itching alone cannot identify infection. \cite{ref198}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Seek assessment if redness spreads, pain or swelling increases, pus appears, or you develop a fever.
\item[] \textbf{REP-045.} \textbf{Myth:} All moles are harmless.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Most moles are benign. Melanoma can arise in an existing mole or appear as a new spot. Change over time is an important clue. \cite{ref339}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Arrange assessment of a new or changing spot that looks unlike your others, or a lesion that repeatedly bleeds.
\item[] \textbf{REP-046.} \textbf{Myth:} Darker skin cannot develop skin cancer.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Skin cancer occurs in every skin tone. In people with darker skin, melanoma can occur on the palms, soles, or under a nail as well as elsewhere. \cite{ref340}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Seek assessment for a changing spot, a new or widening dark nail streak, or a sore that fails to heal.
\item[] \textbf{REP-047.} \textbf{Myth:} Sunburn turns into a healthy tan.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Sunburn and tanning both indicate a response to ultraviolet exposure. A tan that develops after a burn does not undo the injury. \cite{ref319}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Protect burned skin from further sun exposure while it heals, and use shade, clothing, and sunscreen to prevent another burn.
\item[] \textbf{REP-048.} \textbf{Myth:} Older adults cannot learn new skills.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Older adults can learn new skills. The pace of learning varies, and practice, feedback, and accommodations for hearing or vision can help. \cite{ref341}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Choose a meaningful task and allow time for repeated practice. Difficulty with one new task alone does not establish cognitive decline.
\item[] \textbf{REP-049.} \textbf{Myth:} Dietary supplements can reliably stop normal aging.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} No dietary supplement has been shown to stop normal human aging. Supplements can be useful for specific deficiencies or needs, and some interact with medicines. \cite{ref342}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Before taking an anti-aging product, ask what outcome was measured in humans and whether the ingredient is safe with your medicines.
\item[] \textbf{REP-050.} \textbf{Myth:} If a treatment has been used for centuries, that proves it works.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Long use can identify a treatment worth studying. By itself, it cannot distinguish a treatment effect from natural recovery, expectations, or other care received at the same time. \cite{ref205}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Ask what happened to comparable patients who did and did not receive the treatment, and what is known about harms.

\section{Kidney, Nutrition, Cardiovascular, Eye, Breast, Sexual, and First-Aid Myths}
\item[] \textbf{KID-001.} \textbf{Myth:} Drinking enormous quantities of water cleans the kidneys.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Healthy kidneys continually remove waste and adjust how much water leaves the body in urine. Drinking far beyond your needs does not provide an extra cleanse. Rapid excessive intake can dangerously dilute blood sodium. \cite{ref292,ref267,ref268}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Match drinking to your needs and follow any individual fluid advice, including a prescribed target for kidney stones or a restriction for impaired water excretion.
\item[] \textbf{KID-002.} \textbf{Myth:} Clear urine always means perfect hydration.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Clear urine usually means the urine is dilute. Its appearance varies with recent fluid intake and cannot establish that fluid balance is ideal. Normal urine can be yellow. \cite{ref292}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} There is no need to keep drinking until urine is colorless. Discuss persistent excessive thirst or unusually large urine volumes with a clinician.
\item[] \textbf{KID-003.} \textbf{Myth:} Kidney disease always causes kidney-area pain.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Chronic kidney disease often causes no pain, particularly early on. Blood and urine tests can detect changes in filtration and protein leakage before symptoms appear. \cite{ref267}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} If you have diabetes, high blood pressure, or another risk factor, ask when kidney testing is appropriate even if your back feels fine.
\item[] \textbf{KID-004.} \textbf{Myth:} Kidney stones always require surgery.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Many small stones pass on their own. Size, location, pain, infection, and any obstruction affect whether a procedure is needed. \cite{ref183}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Seek urgent care for suspected stone pain with fever or chills, inability to pass urine, or uncontrolled pain.
\item[] \textbf{KID-005.} \textbf{Myth:} All kidney stones are caused by calcium-rich foods.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Stones differ in composition. Even for calcium oxalate stones, calcium from food can bind oxalate in the gut and reduce how much reaches the urine. \cite{ref343}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Ask which type of stone you had before changing your diet. Routine avoidance of calcium-rich foods can work against prevention.
\item[] \textbf{KID-006.} \textbf{Myth:} Normal urination proves the kidneys are normal.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} The amount or frequency of urination can seem normal even when the kidneys filter poorly or leak albumin. These functions require different measurements. \cite{ref267}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} When testing is indicated, a blood creatinine-based estimate of filtration and a urine albumin test provide complementary information.
\item[] \textbf{KID-007.} \textbf{Myth:} You should not swim for 30 minutes after eating.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Evidence does not support a fixed thirty-minute wait between eating and swimming. A large meal may make vigorous swimming uncomfortable. \cite{ref344}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Choose an effort that feels comfortable. Close supervision of children and swimming with a buddy address real drowning risks.
\item[] \textbf{KID-008.} \textbf{Myth:} Swallowed chewing gum remains in your stomach for seven years.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} The digestive tract cannot fully break down the gum base, but it generally moves through the intestines and leaves in stool. It does not normally remain in the stomach. \cite{ref206}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} An accidentally swallowed piece usually needs no treatment. Swallowing large amounts repeatedly can rarely contribute to a blockage, particularly with constipation.
\item[] \textbf{KID-009.} \textbf{Myth:} Holding in a sneeze is always harmless.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Blocking the nose and closing the mouth during a forceful sneeze can briefly raise pressure in the throat. Rare injuries have been documented in case reports, which cannot tell us how often they occur. \cite{ref345}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Let the air escape into a tissue or the crook of your elbow. Sudden neck swelling or difficulty swallowing or breathing afterward needs urgent assessment.
\item[] \textbf{KID-010.} \textbf{Myth:} Sneezing means you are getting a cold.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Sneezing is a reflex to irritation in the nose. A viral cold is one possible cause, alongside pollen, dust, and other irritants. \cite{ref199}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Consider the pattern. Repeated sneezing around a specific exposure, especially with itchy eyes, may suggest allergy.
\item[] \textbf{KID-011.} \textbf{Myth:} Your heart stops when you sneeze.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} The pressure change during a sneeze can briefly alter the timing of a heartbeat. An ordinary sneeze does not stop the heart. \cite{ref346}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} A sneeze alone is no reason to check your pulse. Seek assessment if sneezing is repeatedly accompanied by fainting.
\item[] \textbf{KID-012.} \textbf{Myth:} Humans have only five senses.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} The familiar five omit sensory systems such as balance and proprioception, the sense of where your limbs are without looking. The number depends on how sensory functions are classified. \cite{ref207}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Touching your nose with your eyes closed draws on proprioception. Keeping your balance also uses information from the inner ear, vision, and the body.
\item[] \textbf{KID-013.} \textbf{Myth:} Fingernails continue growing after death.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} After death, skin around the nails can lose moisture and retract, exposing more of the nail. This creates the appearance of growth. \cite{ref208}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} A more prominent nail after death can be explained by changes in the surrounding skin.
\item[] \textbf{KID-014.} \textbf{Myth:} You lose most body heat through your head.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Heat loss depends on which parts of the body are exposed, how well they are insulated, and the surrounding conditions. An uncovered head matters especially when the rest of the body is bundled up. \cite{ref212}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Cover exposed skin and keep clothing dry. A hat is one part of cold protection.
\item[] \textbf{KID-015.} \textbf{Myth:} Sitting on a cold floor causes piles or hemorrhoids.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Cold flooring is not an established cause of hemorrhoids. Straining, constipation, and prolonged sitting on the toilet can increase pressure on the tissues around the anus. \cite{ref347}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Fiber and avoiding prolonged straining may help. Rectal bleeding deserves assessment because several conditions can cause it.
\item[] \textbf{KID-016.} \textbf{Myth:} Sitting on a toilet seat commonly transmits serious infections.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Ordinary contact between intact skin and a toilet seat is an unlikely route for serious infection. Genital herpes, for example, spreads through direct contact with infected skin. Contaminated hands can spread intestinal germs. \cite{ref348,ref315}\newline
