\textbf{Implications:} Wash your hands with soap and water after using the toilet, even when the seat looks clean.
\item[] \textbf{KID-017.} \textbf{Myth:} Drinking water during meals ruins digestion.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Drinking water with a meal does not dilute digestive juices enough to impair ordinary digestion. Water helps with swallowing and moving food through the digestive tract. \cite{ref349}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Drink with meals if comfortable, within any fluid limit prescribed for a medical condition.
\item[] \textbf{KID-018.} \textbf{Myth:} Drinking water immediately after eating causes obesity.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Plain water supplies no calories. Drinking it after a meal can briefly increase scale weight by the weight of the water itself, without adding body fat. \cite{ref349}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Choose the timing that suits you. Substituting plain water for a sugary drink reduces the energy in the meal.
\item[] \textbf{KID-019.} \textbf{Myth:} Fruit must never be eaten after a meal.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Fruit can be eaten before, during, or after a meal. The digestive tract routinely handles mixed meals, and there is no general requirement to eat fruit separately. \cite{ref209}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Choose timing and portions according to appetite, preferences, and any individual dietary advice.
\item[] \textbf{KID-020.} \textbf{Myth:} Fruit eaten with other food ferments dangerously in your stomach.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} The stomach mixes fruit with the rest of a meal and gradually empties into the small intestine. Bacteria in the large intestine can normally ferment carbohydrates that escape digestion and produce gas. \cite{ref209,ref350}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Occasional gas does not mean fruit has spoiled in the stomach. If symptoms recur, note which foods and portions are involved.
\item[] \textbf{KID-021.} \textbf{Myth:} Milk and fish eaten together cause vitiligo.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Vitiligo involves loss of pigment-producing cells and commonly has an autoimmune basis. Eating fish and milk together is not an established cause. \cite{ref351}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} New pale patches can have several causes. Seek assessment instead of eliminating the food combination solely to prevent vitiligo.
\item[] \textbf{KID-022.} \textbf{Myth:} Eating curd or yogurt at night is inherently harmful.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} There is no general medical reason to avoid yogurt after dark. For someone with nighttime reflux, eating close to lying down can matter regardless of the food. \cite{ref352}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} If you tolerate yogurt, choose a time that suits you. If bedtime meals trigger reflux, try allowing more time before lying down.
\item[] \textbf{KID-023.} \textbf{Myth:} Bananas cause colds.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Colds are caused by respiratory viruses. Eating a banana does not cause a viral cold. \cite{ref4}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} A person with a cold can eat bananas if they are comfortable to swallow and otherwise tolerated.
\item[] \textbf{KID-024.} \textbf{Myth:} Citrus fruits cause respiratory infections.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Respiratory infections are caused by infectious organisms. Acidic fruit may sting an already sore throat, which can make the food seem responsible for symptoms. \cite{ref4}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Choose foods and drinks that feel comfortable while you recover. Throat irritation after citrus alone does not establish infection.
\item[] \textbf{KID-025.} \textbf{Myth:} Spicy food causes appendicitis.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Appendicitis is inflammation of the appendix. A blockage can be involved, and in many cases the precise cause is unclear. Spicy food is not an established cause. \cite{ref353}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Worsening abdominal pain, especially pain moving toward the lower right side, warrants prompt medical assessment even if it began after a spicy meal.
\item[] \textbf{KID-026.} \textbf{Myth:} Swallowing fruit seeds commonly causes appendicitis.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Seeds have occasionally been found in removed appendices. Such findings are rare and do not establish swallowed fruit seeds as a common cause of appendicitis. \cite{ref210,ref353}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} An accidentally swallowed small edible seed usually needs no action. Persistent or worsening abdominal pain should be assessed on its own merits.
\item[] \textbf{KID-027.} \textbf{Myth:} Obesity simply means someone lacks self-control.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Body weight is influenced by genetics, appetite regulation, sleep, medications, food access, and other factors. Weight alone cannot reveal a person's effort or habits. \cite{ref354}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} A useful discussion starts with the person's history, health goals, and barriers to care.
\item[] \textbf{KID-028.} \textbf{Myth:} Thin automatically means healthy.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} A lean build does not rule out high blood pressure, diabetes, or nutritional problems. Appearance alone cannot measure these conditions. \cite{ref354,ref300}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Use screening appropriate to your age, history, and risk even when your weight is stable.
\item[] \textbf{KID-029.} \textbf{Myth:} Being overweight automatically means someone is unhealthy.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Higher body weight is associated with increased risk for several illnesses. A weight category alone cannot determine whether an individual currently has a particular illness. \cite{ref354}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Interpret weight alongside blood pressure, laboratory findings when indicated, symptoms, and physical function.
\item[] \textbf{KID-030.} \textbf{Myth:} You can lose substantial body fat from one particular area by exercising it.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Working a muscle strengthens that muscle. Local exercises alone do not reliably produce substantial fat loss from the overlying area. In one trial, abdominal exercises improved endurance without a significant reduction in abdominal fat. \cite{ref305}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Choose exercises for the movement or strength you want to improve. Changes in fat distribution vary between people.
\item[] \textbf{KID-031.} \textbf{Myth:} Starvation diets are the fastest healthy way to lose weight.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Severe food restriction can lower weight quickly while also causing nutrient deficiencies and loss of lean tissue. Specialist programs sometimes use nutritionally complete, very-low-energy diets for defined clinical reasons and limited periods. \cite{ref355}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} If weight loss is appropriate, choose a plan that meets nutritional needs. Seek clinical supervision before undertaking severe energy restriction.
\item[] \textbf{KID-032.} \textbf{Myth:} Carbohydrates eaten at night automatically become fat.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Carbohydrate consumed at night can supply immediate energy or replenish glycogen. The clock does not automatically divert a meal into body fat. Overall intake, activity, and eating patterns matter over time. \cite{ref191}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} If evening eating affects your goals, examine portions, hunger, and sleep patterns before excluding a whole food group.
\item[] \textbf{KID-033.} \textbf{Myth:} Lemon water melts body fat.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Lemon water does not selectively break down body fat. Unsweetened lemon water can reduce energy intake if it replaces a higher-calorie drink. \cite{ref191}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Use lemon for flavor if you enjoy it. Adding sugar changes the energy content.
\item[] \textbf{KID-034.} \textbf{Myth:} Hot water melts abdominal fat.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Drinking hot water does not heat abdominal fat enough to remove it. Water at a comfortable temperature provides hydration without calories. \cite{ref191}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Choose a comfortable drinking temperature. Increasing the temperature offers no established advantage for abdominal fat loss.
\item[] \textbf{KID-035.} \textbf{Myth:} Apple-cider vinegar rapidly burns fat.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Available evidence does not establish apple-cider vinegar as a rapid fat-loss treatment. A widely publicized trial reporting substantial weight loss was retracted over concerns about its data and analysis. \cite{ref356}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Treat claims based on that trial as unsupported. Ordinary culinary use is different from relying on vinegar as a weight-loss treatment.
\item[] \textbf{KID-036.} \textbf{Myth:} Sweating off 1 kg means you have lost 1 kg of fat.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} A sharp drop in weight immediately after heavy sweating largely reflects fluid lost through the skin. The scale measures that water as well as body tissues. \cite{ref292}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Compare weight under similar conditions over time. Deliberately dehydrating yourself can lower the scale reading without comparable fat loss.
\item[] \textbf{KID-037.} \textbf{Myth:} People with diabetes must completely avoid fruit.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Whole fruit can fit into a diabetes meal plan. Fruit contains carbohydrate, and the portion and the rest of the meal affect the glucose response. \cite{ref357}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Choose portions that fit your meal plan. Whole fruit usually provides more fiber than juice.
\item[] \textbf{KID-038.} \textbf{Myth:} Jaggery is safe for diabetes because it is not refined sugar.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Jaggery is a concentrated source of sugar. Its processing and trace minerals do not make its carbohydrate disappear or prevent a rise in glucose. \cite{ref357}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Count jaggery as added sugar when planning a meal or sweetening a drink.
\item[] \textbf{KID-039.} \textbf{Myth:} Dates do not affect blood glucose because they are natural.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Dates contain carbohydrate, including sugars. Their effect on blood glucose depends partly on the number and size eaten and on the rest of the meal. \cite{ref357}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Include the portion of dates when estimating carbohydrate. A large date can contain considerably more than a small one.
\item[] \textbf{KID-040.} \textbf{Myth:} Fruit juice is equivalent to whole fruit.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Even 100 percent juice usually contains less fiber than whole fruit and is easy to drink quickly. Blood glucose can rise faster with juice than with whole fruit. \cite{ref357}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Choose whole fruit regularly and account for the portion if drinking juice.
\item[] \textbf{KID-041.} \textbf{Myth:} Bitter gourd can cure diabetes.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Studies of bitter gourd have not established that it cures diabetes. Eating it as a vegetable and taking a concentrated preparation are different exposures. \cite{ref81}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Do not replace prescribed diabetes treatment with bitter gourd. Mention concentrated products to the clinician managing your glucose.
\item[] \textbf{KID-042.} \textbf{Myth:} Fenugreek can replace diabetes medication.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Evidence for fenugreek as a glucose-lowering supplement is limited. It has not been shown to safely replace prescribed diabetes medication. \cite{ref81}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} If you use a fenugreek supplement, tell your diabetes care team so that it can review interactions and glucose readings.
\item[] \textbf{KID-043.} \textbf{Myth:} Once glucose becomes normal, diabetes has necessarily disappeared.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Normal glucose while taking medication may mean treatment is working. Type 2 diabetes remission generally requires an HbA1c below 6.5 percent measured at least three months after stopping glucose-lowering medication. \cite{ref358}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Discuss medication changes with your clinician. Continued glucose and complication monitoring is recommended even in remission.
\item[] \textbf{KID-044.} \textbf{Myth:} You can reliably judge your glucose from how you feel.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} High or low glucose can occur without obvious symptoms. Sweating, shakiness, or fatigue can also have other causes. \cite{ref359}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Use the monitoring method and low-glucose treatment plan agreed with your care team. Feeling well alone cannot confirm a safe glucose level.
\item[] \textbf{KID-045.} \textbf{Myth:} Only people taking insulin develop hypoglycemia.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Insulin is one cause of hypoglycemia. Medicines that stimulate insulin release, including sulfonylureas, can also cause it, particularly when meals are missed. \cite{ref359}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Ask whether your medicines can cause low glucose and how to treat it. If someone cannot swallow safely, give nothing by mouth and seek emergency help.
\item[] \textbf{KID-046.} \textbf{Myth:} Diabetes affects only blood sugar.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Diabetes can affect blood vessels, nerves, kidneys, and eyes. Blood pressure, cholesterol, and smoking also influence the risk of complications. \cite{ref271}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Include eye, kidney, and foot assessments and cardiovascular risk management in the care plan alongside glucose monitoring.
\item[] \textbf{KID-047.} \textbf{Myth:} Headache is a reliable way to know your blood pressure is high.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} High blood pressure commonly causes no symptoms, and headaches have many causes. Headache intensity cannot tell you the blood pressure reading. \cite{ref360}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Measure blood pressure with a suitable cuff. A sudden, exceptionally severe headache or new weakness or speech difficulty warrants emergency assessment.
\item[] \textbf{KID-048.} \textbf{Myth:} Feeling dizzy means your blood pressure must be low.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Dizziness can accompany a drop in pressure on standing, but it can also come from inner-ear problems, medications, or other causes. A symptom alone cannot establish the reading. \cite{ref361}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Sit or lie down if you feel faint. Seek assessment for recurrent dizziness, especially if it follows a change in medication.
\item[] \textbf{KID-049.} \textbf{Myth:} One high blood-pressure measurement means you definitely have chronic hypertension.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} A measurement can be affected by activity, anxiety, cuff size, and technique. Diagnosis usually relies on repeated properly taken readings, sometimes confirmed with home or ambulatory monitoring. \cite{ref360}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Rest quietly and repeat an unexpected reading. A very high reading with chest pain, severe breathlessness, or new neurological symptoms needs emergency care.
\item[] \textbf{KID-050.} \textbf{Myth:} Blood pressure should always be exactly 120/80 mmHg.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Blood pressure normally varies with activity, posture, and time of day. A reference value is different from an individual treatment target, which depends on clinical circumstances. \cite{ref360}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Discuss your average readings and agreed target with your clinician. Avoid adjusting treatment to chase one exact number.
\item[] \textbf{KID-051.} \textbf{Myth:} If blood pressure becomes normal, antihypertensive medication should automatically be stopped.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} A normal pressure on treatment often indicates that the medication is effective. Pressure can rise again after stopping, and some medicines need a planned dose reduction. \cite{ref360}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Keep taking the prescribed dose unless your clinician changes it. Report repeated low readings or symptoms so the treatment can be reviewed.
\item[] \textbf{KID-052.} \textbf{Myth:} People with hypertension must completely eliminate salt.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Reducing excess sodium can lower blood pressure. Usual dietary targets limit sodium across all foods. They do not require a sodium-free diet. \cite{ref272}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Compare sodium on labels and account for salt in packaged foods, sauces, and meals eaten out. Follow an individual target if one has been prescribed.
\item[] \textbf{KID-053.} \textbf{Myth:} Heart disease always produces warning symptoms.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Coronary disease can develop without symptoms. Some heart attacks cause mild discomfort, breathlessness, or fatigue and may go unrecognized. \cite{ref46,ref362}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Assess cardiovascular risk even when you feel well. Call emergency services for symptoms suggesting a heart attack.
\item[] \textbf{KID-054.} \textbf{Myth:} A normal cholesterol result guarantees you will not have a heart attack.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Cholesterol is one contributor to cardiovascular risk. Age, smoking, diabetes, blood pressure, and family history also matter. A result within the laboratory reference range cannot rule out a future heart attack. \cite{ref46}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Discuss the cholesterol result in the context of your overall risk. New chest discomfort needs assessment regardless of your last result.
\item[] \textbf{KID-055.} \textbf{Myth:} Normal blood pressure guarantees your heart is healthy.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} A blood pressure measurement gives no direct information about coronary artery narrowing, valve function, or an intermittent abnormal heart rhythm. These conditions can occur with normal pressure. \cite{ref46}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Report new exertional chest discomfort, unexplained breathlessness, or fainting even if your home readings are usually normal.
\item[] \textbf{KID-056.} \textbf{Myth:} Glaucoma always causes eye pain.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} The most common form, open-angle glaucoma, usually progresses without pain. An acute angle-closure attack can instead cause sudden eye pain, redness, blurred vision, and nausea. \cite{ref174}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Sudden symptoms of angle closure need emergency eye care. Pain is an unreliable guide to whether routine glaucoma assessment is needed.
\item[] \textbf{KID-057.} \textbf{Myth:} Good vision means you cannot have glaucoma.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Glaucoma can damage peripheral vision gradually while central vision stays sharp. Someone can read an eye chart well and already have optic nerve damage. \cite{ref174}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Follow the eye examination schedule advised for your risk, especially with a family history of glaucoma.
\item[] \textbf{KID-058.} \textbf{Myth:} Glaucoma and cataract are the same disease.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} A cataract is clouding of the eye's lens. Glaucoma damages the optic nerve. Both can affect the same eye and require different treatments. \cite{ref174,ref175}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Ask which condition is limiting vision. Cataract surgery can clear the cloudy lens but cannot restore vision already lost to glaucoma.
\item[] \textbf{KID-059.} \textbf{Myth:} Cataract must become completely ``ripe'' before surgery.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Cataract surgery can be considered when clouding interferes with activities such as reading or driving. There is no requirement to wait until the lens is completely opaque. \cite{ref175}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Discuss how your vision affects daily activities and weigh the likely benefit and risks with the eye surgeon.
\item[] \textbf{KID-060.} \textbf{Myth:} Cataract surgery permanently restores youthful vision in every patient.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Cataract surgery replaces the cloudy lens with an artificial one. The result depends in part on the retina, optic nerve, and type of implant. Some people still need glasses. \cite{ref175}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Discuss expected near and distance vision and any other eye disease before choosing an implant.
\item[] \textbf{KID-061.} \textbf{Myth:} Glaucoma damage can routinely be reversed with eye drops.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Glaucoma drops lower eye pressure to slow further optic nerve damage. Vision already lost to glaucoma generally cannot be restored. \cite{ref174}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Continue prescribed drops and follow-up even if vision feels the same. Examinations help determine whether the treatment is working.
\item[] \textbf{KID-062.} \textbf{Myth:} Eye pressure alone determines whether someone has glaucoma.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Some people have elevated eye pressure without optic nerve damage, while others develop glaucoma at typical pressure readings. Examination of the nerve and visual field adds important information. \cite{ref174}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} An elevated measurement deserves follow-up. Ask how the pressure compares with the other findings.
\item[] \textbf{KID-063.} \textbf{Myth:} Normal eye pressure means glaucoma is impossible.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Normal-tension glaucoma causes characteristic optic nerve damage despite pressure readings within the usual range. A single reading can also miss fluctuations. \cite{ref174}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Keep recommended follow-up for suspicious nerve or visual field findings even when the measured pressure is normal.
\item[] \textbf{KID-064.} \textbf{Myth:} Carrots can correct refractive errors and eliminate glasses.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Vitamin A supports normal vision, and deficiency can impair vision in dim light. Nearsightedness and farsightedness arise from the way the eye focuses light. Eating carrots cannot correct that focusing error. \cite{ref306}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Eat a varied diet and use prescribed lenses for clear vision.
\item[] \textbf{KID-065.} \textbf{Myth:} Wearing somebody else's glasses permanently damages your eyes.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Another person's glasses may blur vision or cause temporary strain or headache. Brief use by an adult does not ordinarily injure the eye. Children who need correction should wear their own prescription to support visual development. \cite{ref306}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Use your own correction for driving and other activities requiring clear vision. Arrange an eye exam for persistent blur.
\item[] \textbf{KID-066.} \textbf{Myth:} Breast pain usually means breast cancer.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Breast pain is common and often has a benign cause, including cyclical hormonal changes. Pain by itself is an uncommon presentation of breast cancer, although it cannot exclude it. \cite{ref311}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Have persistent, localized, or unexplained pain assessed, especially with a new lump or skin or nipple change.
\item[] \textbf{KID-067.} \textbf{Myth:} Breast cancer always presents as a lump.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Breast cancer can present with skin dimpling, a new nipple change, or swelling. Some cancers are found on imaging before a lump can be felt. \cite{ref363}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Report a new persistent change even when you cannot feel a lump.
\item[] \textbf{KID-068.} \textbf{Myth:} A movable breast lump can never be malignant.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Some benign breast lumps move readily under the fingers. Mobility alone cannot establish what a lump is, and a malignant lump can also be mobile. \cite{ref311}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Arrange an examination for a new lump. Depending on findings, imaging and sometimes a biopsy may be needed.
\item[] \textbf{KID-069.} \textbf{Myth:} Breast cancer occurs only after menopause.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Breast cancer risk generally increases with age, but breast cancer also occurs before menopause. \cite{ref363,ref311}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Have a new lump or persistent breast change assessed at any age.
\item[] \textbf{KID-070.} \textbf{Myth:} Breastfeeding guarantees protection against breast cancer.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Breastfeeding is associated with a lower average risk of breast cancer. It cannot guarantee protection for an individual. \cite{ref364}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Seek assessment of a persistent lump during or after breastfeeding and follow screening advice for your age and risk.
\item[] \textbf{KID-071.} \textbf{Myth:} Breast injury causes breast cancer.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} An injury can cause bruising or fat necrosis, a benign change that can feel like a lump. An injury can also draw attention to a lump that was already present. Breast injury is not an established cause of breast cancer. \cite{ref365}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Have a persistent or changing lump assessed even when it first appeared after a knock.
\item[] \textbf{KID-072.} \textbf{Myth:} Deodorant or antiperspirant causes breast cancer.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Available human evidence does not establish that using underarm deodorants or antiperspirants causes breast cancer. \cite{ref16,ref312}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Choose a product you tolerate. If an imaging center asks you to avoid it on the day of mammography, follow its instructions because residue can affect the image.
\item[] \textbf{KID-073.} \textbf{Myth:} Mammography detects every breast cancer.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Mammography can detect many cancers before symptoms appear, but some cancers are missed. Dense breast tissue can make a tumor harder to see. \cite{ref312}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Attend recommended screening and report new breast changes between appointments.
\item[] \textbf{KID-074.} \textbf{Myth:} A normal mammogram means a new breast symptom can always be ignored.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} A screening mammogram describes what was visible at the time. Some abnormalities require an examination, targeted imaging, or biopsy even after a normal screening result. \cite{ref312,ref363}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Contact a clinician about a new lump, skin dimpling, or new nipple retraction rather than waiting for the next screen.
\item[] \textbf{KID-075.} \textbf{Myth:} BI-RADS 3 means ``early cancer.''\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} BI-RADS 3 means a finding is probably benign, with an expected likelihood of malignancy of no more than two percent. It is an imaging assessment, not a cancer stage. \cite{ref274,ref366}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Keep the recommended short-interval imaging appointment, often at six months. Comparison shows whether the finding remains stable.
\item[] \textbf{KID-076.} \textbf{Myth:} Masturbation causes blindness.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Masturbation is a common sexual behavior. There is no evidence that it causes blindness. \cite{ref367}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} A new visual problem deserves assessment on its own merits. Sudden loss of vision requires urgent care.
\item[] \textbf{KID-077.} \textbf{Myth:} Masturbation causes permanent physical weakness.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Some people feel relaxed or briefly tired after orgasm. Masturbation does not cause permanent loss of physical strength. \cite{ref367}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Persistent fatigue deserves assessment for other causes, especially if it interferes with daily activities.
\item[] \textbf{KID-078.} \textbf{Myth:} Semen loss inherently causes severe nutritional depletion.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Semen contains small amounts of nutrients. The quantities lost through ordinary ejaculation are too small to cause nutritional depletion. \cite{ref367}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} There is no nutritional need to replace semen with supplements or special foods. Discuss ongoing weakness as a separate symptom.
\item[] \textbf{KID-079.} \textbf{Myth:} Vasectomy causes impotence.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Vasectomy interrupts the tubes that carry sperm into semen. It does not ordinarily change testosterone production or the ability to have an erection or orgasm. \cite{ref275}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Follow postoperative instructions while recovering. Persistent pain or difficulty with erections warrants review.
\item[] \textbf{KID-080.} \textbf{Myth:} Vasectomy immediately makes a man sterile.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Sperm can remain in the reproductive tract after vasectomy. Time elapsed or the number of ejaculations alone cannot confirm success. \cite{ref275,ref368}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Use another contraceptive method until the clinician confirms that a post-vasectomy semen test meets the clearance criteria.
\item[] \textbf{KID-081.} \textbf{Myth:} Contraceptive pills protect against sexually transmitted infections.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Contraceptive pills prevent pregnancy but provide no barrier to infections transmitted during sexual contact. \cite{ref323,ref176}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Condoms reduce the risk of many sexually transmitted infections. Ask about testing according to exposures and local guidance.
\item[] \textbf{KID-082.} \textbf{Myth:} You can always recognize an STI by symptoms.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Many sexually transmitted infections cause no symptoms, and different infections can cause similar symptoms. Testing is often the only way to identify an infection. \cite{ref176}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Seek advice after a possible exposure even if you feel well. The appropriate tests and timing depend on the exposure.
\item[] \textbf{KID-083.} \textbf{Myth:} Washing immediately after intercourse prevents pregnancy.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} External washing or vaginal douching after intercourse cannot reliably remove sperm or prevent pregnancy. Douching can also disturb the usual vaginal bacteria. \cite{ref321}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} If pregnancy is unwanted after unprotected intercourse, seek prompt advice about emergency contraception.
\item[] \textbf{KID-084.} \textbf{Myth:} Urinating after intercourse prevents pregnancy.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Urine leaves the body through the urethra, a separate opening from the vagina. Urinating cannot flush sperm from the reproductive tract. \cite{ref369}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} If contraception failed or was not used, ask promptly about emergency contraception if pregnancy is unwanted.
\item[] \textbf{KID-085.} \textbf{Myth:} Infertility is usually the woman's problem.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Infertility can involve factors affecting either partner, both partners, or no identifiable cause after evaluation. An individual's contribution cannot be inferred from sex alone. \cite{ref187}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} When a couple is assessed, evaluating both partners can identify treatable causes and avoid unnecessary delay.
\item[] \textbf{KID-086.} \textbf{Myth:} Put butter or toothpaste on a burn.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Butter and toothpaste have no established role in first aid for a thermal burn. Applying them can interfere with cooling and assessment. \cite{ref370}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Cool the burned area promptly under cool running water for about 20 minutes while keeping the rest of the person warm. Seek urgent care for deep, extensive, or serious burns.
\item[] \textbf{KID-087.} \textbf{Myth:} Put ice directly on a burn.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Direct ice can further injure burned skin. Cool running water removes heat without the risk of freezing the tissue. \cite{ref370}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Use cool running water for about 20 minutes. Remove nearby jewelry or loose clothing if easy to do, leaving material stuck to the skin in place.
\item[] \textbf{KID-088.} \textbf{Myth:} Make someone vomit whenever they have swallowed poison.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Inducing vomiting can cause choking or a second exposure of the throat to a corrosive substance. The right response depends on what was swallowed. \cite{ref180}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Contact a poison service promptly and keep the container available. Call emergency services immediately if the person collapses, has a seizure, cannot be awakened, or has trouble breathing.
\item[] \textbf{KID-089.} \textbf{Myth:} Sucking venom from a snakebite removes the poison.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Mouth suction and suction devices do not reliably remove a clinically useful amount of snake venom. Attempting suction can injure tissue or delay treatment. \cite{ref269,ref371}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Move away from the snake, keep the person and bitten limb still, and arrange urgent transport for medical care.
\item[] \textbf{KID-090.} \textbf{Myth:} Cutting a snakebite helps venom escape.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Cutting a bite does not drain venom effectively. It creates another wound and can worsen bleeding, particularly if venom has impaired clotting. \cite{ref269,ref371}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Leave the bite site alone, immobilize the limb, and seek emergency care promptly.
\item[] \textbf{KID-091.} \textbf{Myth:} A tight tourniquet should routinely be tied above every snakebite.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} A tight tourniquet cuts off circulation and can cause severe local injury. Regional advice on pressure immobilization depends on the snake and setting and is distinct from tying a tight band. \cite{ref269,ref371}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Keep the person still, remove constricting jewelry before swelling develops, and obtain emergency help. Follow the local emergency team's instructions.
\item[] \textbf{KID-092.} \textbf{Myth:} Someone who is fainting should be forced to drink water immediately.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Someone who is losing consciousness may be unable to swallow safely. Forced drinks can enter the airway. \cite{ref372}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Help the person lie down and check responsiveness and breathing. If unresponsive but breathing normally, use the recovery position when safe and call emergency services. If breathing is absent or abnormal, call and start CPR.
\item[] \textbf{KID-093.} \textbf{Myth:} A person having a heart attack should drive themselves to hospital.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} During a heart attack, sudden collapse or an abnormal heart rhythm can make driving unsafe. An ambulance team can begin assessment and treatment en route. \cite{ref362}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Call local emergency services promptly for suspected heart attack symptoms and follow the dispatcher's instructions.
\item[] \textbf{KID-094.} \textbf{Myth:} If someone can move an injured limb, it cannot be fractured.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} A person may still move or bear weight on a limb with a fracture, especially if the bone ends remain aligned. Movement alone cannot rule it out. \cite{ref200}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Support the injured limb without repeatedly testing it. Seek assessment for marked tenderness, swelling, deformity, or difficulty using it; an open wound over a suspected fracture needs urgent care.
\item[] \textbf{KID-095.} \textbf{Myth:} Drinking cold water causes a respiratory infection.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Respiratory infections require exposure to an infectious organism. The temperature of drinking water does not cause a viral cold. \cite{ref4}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Choose a drink temperature that feels comfortable, particularly if your throat is already sore.
\item[] \textbf{KID-096.} \textbf{Myth:} Ice cream directly causes tonsillitis.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Tonsillitis usually results from a virus and sometimes from bacteria. Ice cream does not itself cause that infection. Cold foods or drinks may temporarily soothe a sore throat. \cite{ref373}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Seek assessment if symptoms are severe or persistent. Difficulty breathing or inability to swallow saliva requires emergency care.
\item[] \textbf{KID-097.} \textbf{Myth:} Bathing immediately after eating is medically dangerous.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} There is no established requirement for a healthy person to delay an ordinary bath after eating. Very hot baths can affect blood pressure and cause lightheadedness, a separate issue from digestion. \cite{ref374}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Use a comfortable water temperature and stand up slowly if prone to dizziness. If you feel faint, avoid getting into the bath while symptoms persist.
\item[] \textbf{KID-098.} \textbf{Myth:} Eating rice at night inevitably causes obesity.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Rice supplies carbohydrate and energy at any time of day. Eating it in the evening does not inevitably cause weight gain. Portion size and the overall eating pattern matter. \cite{ref191}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Choose a portion that fits the meal and your needs. Pairing rice with vegetables and a protein source can make a more balanced meal.
\item[] \textbf{KID-099.} \textbf{Myth:} Ghee is harmless in unlimited amounts because it is traditional.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Ghee is a concentrated source of energy and saturated fat. Frequent large amounts can increase saturated fat intake substantially. Replacing some saturated fat with unsaturated fats can lower LDL cholesterol. \cite{ref375}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Use a quantity suited to the dish and consider unsaturated vegetable oils for some meals.
\item[] \textbf{KID-100.} \textbf{Myth:} Honey becomes poisonous when added to warm water.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} There is no established evidence that stirring honey into warm water makes it poisonous. Heating can change concentrations of compounds such as hydroxymethylfurfural, but detecting a compound does not establish harm at ordinary food exposures. \cite{ref376,ref377}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Honey still counts as added sugar. Keep honey out of foods and drinks for infants under one year because of the risk of infant botulism, regardless of drink temperature.
\item[] \textbf{KID-101.} \textbf{Myth:} Papaya is universally dangerous during pregnancy.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} The evidence does not support calling all papaya dangerous during pregnancy. In a rat study, researchers observed no maternal or fetal toxicity with ripe papaya. Crude papaya latex caused contractions in isolated rat uterine muscle. Those findings do not establish the risk of eating unripe fruit in human pregnancy. \cite{ref276}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Distinguish ordinary ripe fruit from concentrated latex or herbal preparations. Human pregnancy safety data are limited, especially for unripe papaya and extracts; discuss their use with a prenatal clinician.
\item[] \textbf{KID-102.} \textbf{Myth:} Turmeric can cure cancer.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Curcumin, a component of turmeric, has been studied in the laboratory and in small human studies. These findings have not established that turmeric or curcumin cures cancer. \cite{ref378}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Ordinary use as a spice differs from taking a concentrated supplement. Discuss supplements with the oncology team before using them during treatment.
\item[] \textbf{KID-103.} \textbf{Myth:} Ayurvedic or herbal products are automatically safe because they are natural.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Safety depends on the specific preparation, dose, and other medicines a person takes. Tests of some Ayurvedic products have found lead, mercury, or arsenic. These findings concern the products tested and cannot be generalized to every preparation. \cite{ref115,ref116,ref117}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Tell the clinician or pharmacist the exact product and dose, especially before surgery or when starting another medicine.

\section{Additional Common Myths}

The following entries address additional claims encountered in public-health discussions and reference collections. Their inclusion does not imply that every claim is equally prevalent or that a single source represents all communities; each claim should still be interpreted in its clinical and cultural context \cite{ref16,ref17,ref18,ref36,ref15,ref59,ref60,ref61}.
\item[] \textbf{CAN-001.} \textbf{Myth:} Cell phones cause cancer.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Large human studies have generally found no association between ordinary mobile phone use and brain tumors. The evidence does not establish that phone use causes cancer, although individual studies have limitations in measuring long-term exposure. \cite{ref379}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} For someone who prefers to reduce exposure, speaker mode or a headset increases the distance between the handset and the head.
\item[] \textbf{CAN-002.} \textbf{Myth:} Power lines directly cause cancer.\newline
\textbf{Status:} Uncertain.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Some studies have found an association between higher long-term residential magnetic field exposure and childhood leukemia. Bias, confounding, or chance could contribute, and the evidence has not established that power lines cause leukemia. \cite{ref380}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} The association warrants accurate reporting. Living near a power line alone cannot identify the cause of an individual child's cancer.
\item[] \textbf{CAN-003.} \textbf{Myth:} A positive attitude determines whether a person survives cancer.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} A person's outlook can affect coping and quality of life. Evidence does not show that staying positive determines whether a cancer responds to treatment or whether the person survives. \cite{ref16}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Allow people to express fear, sadness, or hope without making them responsible for the course of their cancer.
\item[] \textbf{CAN-004.} \textbf{Myth:} No family history means no cancer risk.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Most cancers arise in people who have not inherited a high-risk cancer variant. Risk also reflects age, exposures, and genetic changes acquired over a lifetime. \cite{ref16}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Follow applicable screening guidance even without a family history. A strong family history may change which screening is appropriate.
\item[] \textbf{CAN-005.} \textbf{Myth:} Microwave-safe plastic containers always leach toxic cancer-causing chemicals.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Microwave-safe labeling refers to the container's intended conditions of use. It does not mean every plastic can be heated safely or that ordinary use of a suitable container necessarily exposes someone to cancer-causing doses. \cite{ref6}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Follow the container's heating instructions. Transfer food from packaging of uncertain suitability into microwave-safe glass or ceramic.
\item[] \textbf{CAN-006.} \textbf{Myth:} Cancer treatment is always worse than the disease.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Treatments differ in their purposes, benefits, and side effects. Some can cure cancer, others extend life, and some relieve symptoms. The balance depends on the disease and what matters to the individual. \cite{ref16}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Ask about the expected course with and without treatment, likely side effects, and options for managing them.
\item[] \textbf{CAN-007.} \textbf{Myth:} Cancer screening always causes more harm than good.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Some screening programs reduce deaths from the cancer they target in defined groups. Potential harms include false alarms, invasive follow-up, and detection of cancers that would never cause illness. \cite{ref185}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Use recommendations for the specific test, age, and risk group. New symptoms need diagnostic assessment even between screening visits.
\item[] \textbf{CAN-008.} \textbf{Myth:} A Pap smear detects ovarian cancer.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} A Pap test samples cells from the cervix to look for changes that can lead to cervical cancer. It is not a reliable test for ovarian cancer. \cite{ref381}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Discuss persistent new bloating, pelvic discomfort, or feeling full unusually quickly even if a recent Pap result was normal.
\item[] \textbf{CAN-009.} \textbf{Myth:} Only older women develop ovarian cancer.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Ovarian cancer becomes more common with age, but it can also occur in younger people. Some rare ovarian germ cell tumors disproportionately affect adolescents and young adults. \cite{ref382,ref381}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Persistent unexplained pelvic or abdominal symptoms deserve assessment regardless of age.
\item[] \textbf{CAN-010.} \textbf{Myth:} Vaping is completely harmless.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Vape aerosol can contain nicotine, particles, metals, and other substances. Exposure varies by product. The absence of burning tobacco does not make inhaling the aerosol harmless. \cite{ref383,ref278}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} People who do not smoke should avoid starting to vape, particularly during adolescence or pregnancy.
\item[] \textbf{CAN-011.} \textbf{Myth:} Nicotine causes most of the disease caused by smoking.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Nicotine drives dependence and has health effects of its own. Much of the cancer and lung disease caused by smoking results from inhaling the toxic products of tobacco combustion. \cite{ref278,ref384}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Medicinal nicotine used for quitting avoids smoke exposure, so its risks differ substantially from those of continued cigarette smoking.
\item[] \textbf{CAN-012.} \textbf{Myth:} Vaping always causes popcorn lung.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Popcorn lung is a nickname for bronchiolitis obliterans, a rare condition involving scarring of small airways. Inhaling certain flavoring chemicals in workplaces has been linked to it. That does not make it an inevitable outcome of vaping. \cite{ref278,ref383}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Persistent cough or breathlessness warrants medical assessment. Describe any vaping and occupational exposures rather than assuming a specific diagnosis.
\item[] \textbf{CAN-013.} \textbf{Myth:} Switching completely from cigarettes to vaping provides no health benefit.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Adults who smoke can reduce exposure to many harmful chemicals by switching completely from cigarettes to e-cigarettes. The size of the long-term health benefit remains uncertain, and continuing to smoke while vaping preserves important risks. \cite{ref383,ref278}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} For an adult trying to quit smoking, discuss cessation support. The evidence on substitution gives a nonsmoker no health reason to start vaping.
\item[] \textbf{CAN-014.} \textbf{Myth:} Everyone must sleep exactly eight hours.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Sleep needs change with age and vary among individuals. Eight hours is a useful approximation for many adults, while children and adolescents generally need more. \cite{ref385}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Consider daytime alertness and sleep quality alongside hours slept. Persistent sleepiness despite adequate opportunity to sleep deserves assessment.
\item[] \textbf{CAN-015.} \textbf{Myth:} Extra weekend sleep completely repairs chronic sleep debt.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Extra sleep can reduce sleepiness after a short night. In a controlled study, weekend recovery sleep did not prevent all the metabolic changes when participants returned to restricted sleep. \cite{ref386}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Allow enough sleep on ordinary work or school nights rather than relying entirely on a weekend catch-up.
\item[] \textbf{CAN-016.} \textbf{Myth:} Muscle turns into fat when a person stops exercising.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Muscle and fat are different tissues. Stopping training can reduce muscle size, while changes in activity and energy intake can separately increase fat stores. One tissue does not turn into the other. \cite{ref387}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} After a break, return to activity progressively and choose a training load that fits your current strength.
\item[] \textbf{CAN-017.} \textbf{Myth:} Everyone must have one bowel movement every day.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Healthy bowel frequency varies. Constipation can involve hard stools, straining, or incomplete emptying as well as infrequent bowel movements. \cite{ref388}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} A comfortable, stable pattern need not be changed to achieve a daily bowel movement. Seek advice for a persistent change or blood in the stool.
\item[] \textbf{CAN-018.} \textbf{Myth:} Organic food is always more nutritious than conventional food.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Organic labeling describes how a food was produced. Comparisons find differences in some nutrients, but no consistent nutritional advantage for every organic food over its conventional counterpart. \cite{ref184}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Choose a varied diet using foods that suit your preferences and budget. Both organic and conventional produce can contribute.
\item[] \textbf{CAN-019.} \textbf{Myth:} Red meat must always be avoided.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Red meat supplies protein, iron, and other nutrients. Cancer prevention guidance recommends limiting red meat and eating little, if any, processed meat. A universal ban on all red meat does not follow from this evidence. \cite{ref389}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} If you eat meat, vary protein sources and keep processed meats such as bacon and sausages occasional.
\item[] \textbf{CAN-020.} \textbf{Myth:} Coffee permanently stunts growth.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Evidence does not establish that drinking coffee permanently reduces adult height. Caffeine can interfere with sleep and cause jitteriness, especially in children. \cite{ref390}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Consider caffeine content, age, and sleep when choosing drinks for a child.
\item[] \textbf{CAN-021.} \textbf{Myth:} Only girls develop eating disorders.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Eating disorders affect people of all genders. Symptoms in boys and men can go unrecognized, including restrictive eating or compulsive exercise related to muscularity concerns. \cite{ref186}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Take a marked change in eating, exercise, or distress about body image seriously regardless of gender.
\item[] \textbf{CAN-022.} \textbf{Myth:} You can identify an eating disorder from someone's weight alone.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} A person can have a serious eating disorder at a low, average, or high weight. Behaviors, distress, physical findings, and changes over time all inform assessment. \cite{ref186}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Seek help for restriction, binge eating, purging, or compulsive exercise even if weight looks typical.
\item[] \textbf{CAN-023.} \textbf{Myth:} Eating disorders are a lifestyle choice.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Eating disorders are illnesses influenced by biological, psychological, and social factors. Behaviors that appear voluntary can become difficult to interrupt and can cause serious harm. \cite{ref186}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Offer support without blame and encourage assessment by someone experienced in eating disorders.
\item[] \textbf{CAN-024.} \textbf{Myth:} Eating disorders usually resolve without professional treatment.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} The course varies, and recovery is possible. Waiting for an eating disorder to resolve can allow medical or psychological complications to worsen. Early treatment improves the chance of recovery. \cite{ref186}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Seek assessment when symptoms are suspected. Treatment may include nutritional support, psychotherapy, and medical monitoring.
\item[] \textbf{INF-001.} \textbf{Myth:} ``Starve a fever, feed a cold'' is a sound medical rule.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Neither a cold nor a fever calls for deliberate fasting. Appetite may fall during an illness, while fever and sweating can increase fluid needs. \cite{ref391,ref295}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Offer fluids and food as tolerated. Seek advice if the person cannot keep fluids down or shows signs of dehydration.
\item[] \textbf{INF-002.} \textbf{Myth:} Milk or dairy products increase mucus during a cold.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} In a study of adults exposed to a cold virus, dairy intake was not associated with greater measured nasal secretions. Milk can leave a temporary coating sensation that feels like thicker mucus. \cite{ref279}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} People who tolerate dairy need not avoid it because of a cold. A milk allergy is a separate reason for avoidance.
\item[] \textbf{INF-003.} \textbf{Myth:} Vitamin C prevents all colds.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Regular vitamin C supplementation has not prevented colds in the general population. Trials found a modest reduction in cold duration with regular use, with different prevention findings in people under brief, extreme physical stress. \cite{ref280}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Do not count on starting vitamin C after symptoms appear to reliably shorten a cold.
\item[] \textbf{INF-004.} \textbf{Myth:} Cranberry juice cures an established urinary tract infection.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Cranberry products may help prevent recurrent urinary infections in some groups. Prevention studies do not show that juice treats an infection already causing symptoms. \cite{ref392,ref281}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Seek advice for urinary symptoms. Fever, flank pain, vomiting, or symptoms during pregnancy warrant prompt assessment.
\item[] \textbf{INF-005.} \textbf{Myth:} Drinking urine is therapeutic.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Drinking urine has no established therapeutic benefit. Urine contains substances the kidneys are excreting, and its composition varies with hydration, illness, and medicines. \cite{ref393}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Use safe drinking water for hydration and seek appropriate treatment for the condition of concern.
\item[] \textbf{INF-006.} \textbf{Myth:} Healthy urine is completely sterile.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Sensitive methods have detected bacteria in bladder urine from some adults without a symptomatic urinary infection. A collected sample can also acquire bacteria from surrounding skin. \cite{ref282}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Detecting bacteria alone does not establish a urinary infection that needs antibiotics. Symptoms, collection method, and clinical circumstances matter.
\item[] \textbf{INF-007.} \textbf{Myth:} Antibacterial soap is always better than plain soap.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} For routine washing at home, consumer antibacterial soaps have not been shown to prevent illness better than plain soap and water. Clinical antisepsis is a different use. \cite{ref394}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Wash thoroughly with plain soap and running water, especially after using the toilet and before preparing food.
\item[] \textbf{INF-008.} \textbf{Myth:} Chicken soup cures a cold.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Chicken soup supplies fluid and nourishment, and a warm broth may soothe a sore throat. It has not been shown to eliminate the viruses that cause colds. \cite{ref391}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Choose soup if it helps you eat or drink comfortably. Seek care for breathing difficulty or symptoms that worsen after initial improvement.
\item[] \textbf{INF-009.} \textbf{Myth:} Over-the-counter cough and cold medicines cure respiratory viral infections.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Over-the-counter cold products can temporarily relieve selected symptoms. They do not eliminate the virus, and some products are unsuitable for young children. \cite{ref391,ref43}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Check age restrictions and active ingredients. Combining products with the same ingredient, such as acetaminophen, can lead to an overdose.
\item[] \textbf{INF-010.} \textbf{Myth:} Zinc or other supplements are universally risk-free for colds.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Oral zinc can cause nausea and interact with medicines. Excessive long-term intake can cause copper deficiency. Intranasal zinc products have been linked to loss of smell. \cite{ref195}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Check the formulation and dose before using a supplement. Avoid intranasal zinc remedies for a cold.
\item[] \textbf{INF-011.} \textbf{Myth:} Every fever should be treated until body temperature is completely normal.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} For many routine infections, the purpose of fever medicine is comfort. A temperature does not have to return to normal after each dose. Age, alertness, breathing, and hydration help guide care. \cite{ref8,ref295}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Follow the correct dose and interval. An infant under three months with a rectal temperature of 38 degrees Celsius or higher needs prompt assessment.
\item[] \textbf{INF-012.} \textbf{Myth:} Pulling a gray hair makes more gray hair grow in its place.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Hair color is determined within each follicle. Plucking one gray hair does not change pigment production in neighboring follicles. The replacement hair will usually still be gray. \cite{ref395}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Repeated plucking can thin the area. Trimming is an alternative if an individual hair bothers you.

\section{Important High-Impact Myths}

The following high-impact myths are included because they can lead to preventable medication errors, delayed care, or avoidable harm.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-001.} \textbf{Myth:} The flu vaccine gives you influenza.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Injected influenza vaccines cannot cause influenza. The nasal spray uses weakened viruses and can cause mild nasal symptoms. Soreness, fatigue, or a brief fever after vaccination may also occur. \cite{ref40,ref396}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Vaccination takes time to provide protection, and other respiratory viruses can cause similar symptoms. Illness after a dose does not by itself establish that the vaccine caused flu.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-002.} \textbf{Myth:} A COVID-19 vaccine changes a person's DNA or causes infertility.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} COVID-19 vaccines do not alter a person's DNA. Studies have found no evidence that vaccination causes infertility in women or men. Temporary changes in menstrual timing are distinct from infertility. \cite{ref41}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Fertility concerns can be discussed with a clinician using evidence about the specific vaccine and personal health circumstances.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-003.} \textbf{Myth:} Generic medicines are weaker than brand-name medicines.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} An approved generic must match the reference medicine in active ingredient, strength, dosage form, and route and meet applicable equivalence standards. Color, shape, and some inactive ingredients may differ. \cite{ref42}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Check the active ingredient and strength with a pharmacist if a refill looks different or an excipient matters for an allergy.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-004.} \textbf{Myth:} Paracetamol or acetaminophen is harmless at any dose.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Paracetamol and acetaminophen are two names for the same medicine. Excess can cause severe liver injury, and early symptoms may be mild or absent. \cite{ref43}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Check all pain and cold remedies for the same ingredient. If an overdose may have occurred, contact a poison service or emergency department immediately, even if the person feels well.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-005.} \textbf{Myth:} If a prescribed medicine makes you feel better, you can stop it immediately.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} How to stop depends on the medicine and why it was prescribed. Some are taken only as needed, some control a condition while in use, and others need gradual reduction to avoid withdrawal. \cite{ref397,ref272}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Follow the instructions for that specific medicine. Ask the prescriber or pharmacist before stopping a scheduled treatment solely because symptoms improved.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-006.} \textbf{Myth:} Harder tooth-brushing cleans better.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Plaque removal depends on reaching tooth surfaces and the gumline with a suitable technique. Excessive pressure can injure gums and contribute to tooth wear. \cite{ref44}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Use a soft-bristled brush and gentle pressure. Ask a dentist to demonstrate technique if brushing causes persistent discomfort.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-007.} \textbf{Myth:} Fluoride toothpaste is dangerous and should be avoided.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Fluoride toothpaste helps prevent tooth decay. The amount matters for young children who may swallow it while teeth are developing. \cite{ref45}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Use a rice-grain-sized smear for children under three and a pea-sized amount from ages three to six. Supervise brushing and encourage spitting as the child learns.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-008.} \textbf{Myth:} Baby teeth do not matter because they eventually fall out.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Primary teeth help children chew and speak and hold space for permanent teeth. Decay can cause pain or infection well before a tooth is ready to fall out. \cite{ref398}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Start caring for teeth when the first one appears. Have tooth pain, swelling, or visible decay assessed.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-009.} \textbf{Myth:} Tilt the head backward during a nosebleed.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Tilting the head back lets blood run into the throat and makes the bleeding harder to judge. \cite{ref181}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Sit upright, lean forward, and pinch the soft part of the nose continuously for 10 to 15 minutes while breathing through the mouth. Seek urgent care for heavy or persistent bleeding or difficulty breathing.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-010.} \textbf{Myth:} People with mental illness are generally dangerous.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Most people living with mental illness are not violent. A diagnosis alone is a poor basis for predicting whether a particular person presents a danger. \cite{ref399}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Respond to specific behavior and immediate threats in their context. Avoid assuming a person is dangerous because they disclose a diagnosis.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-011.} \textbf{Myth:} Antidepressants are addictive in the same way as recreational drugs.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Antidepressants do not typically produce the craving and compulsive use associated with addiction. The body can adapt to them, and stopping or reducing the dose can produce withdrawal symptoms that may be distressing. \cite{ref397}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Plan dose reductions with the prescriber. The pace may need adjusting if symptoms develop.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-012.} \textbf{Myth:} Therapy is only for people who are weak or unable to cope.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Psychotherapy is used to treat mental disorders and to help people manage grief, major changes, or relationship difficulties. Seeking it says nothing about a person's strength. \cite{ref400}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Consider therapy when distress affects everyday life or when structured support could help you work toward a specific goal.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-013.} \textbf{Myth:} Spicy food causes peptic ulcers.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Most peptic ulcers are caused by infection with Helicobacter pylori or by medicines such as ibuprofen and other nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs. Spicy food can aggravate discomfort in some people without causing the ulcer. \cite{ref401}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Persistent upper abdominal pain warrants assessment. Vomiting blood or passing black, tarry stools needs urgent medical care.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-014.} \textbf{Myth:} Emergency contraception is the same as an abortion.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Emergency contraception prevents pregnancy after unprotected intercourse. Emergency contraceptive pills mainly delay or prevent ovulation. Neither the pills nor a copper intrauterine device used for emergency contraception ends an established pregnancy. \cite{ref402}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} If pregnancy is unwanted after unprotected intercourse, seek prompt advice. The available options depend partly on how much time has passed.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-015.} \textbf{Myth:} Exercise or sexual intercourse routinely causes miscarriage in an uncomplicated pregnancy.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} In an uncomplicated pregnancy, ordinary physical activity and sexual intercourse have not been shown to cause miscarriage. Many early losses result from problems in the development of the pregnancy. \cite{ref254,ref403}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Follow individualized advice if there is bleeding or a pregnancy complication. A miscarriage after routine activity is not evidence that the activity caused it.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-016.} \textbf{Myth:} A person who is choking should be given water.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Water cannot dislodge an object blocking the airway, and a choking person may be unable to swallow. Someone who can cough forcefully should be encouraged to keep coughing. \cite{ref404}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} If the person cannot cough effectively, speak, or breathe, have someone call emergency services immediately and begin age-appropriate choking first aid. Follow dispatcher instructions. If the person becomes unresponsive and is not breathing normally, start CPR.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-017.} \textbf{Myth:} More medical screening is always better.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Screening helps when finding disease early improves outcomes enough to outweigh the harms of testing and follow-up. Testing a low-risk group can generate false alarms, unnecessary procedures, or treatment of conditions that would never cause symptoms. \cite{ref185}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Ask who benefits from the test, how often it is recommended, and what would happen after an abnormal result.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-018.} \textbf{Myth:} A normal test result proves that serious disease is impossible.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} A normal result reduces the likelihood of the condition a test was designed to detect. How much reassurance it provides depends on test sensitivity, timing, and the person's symptoms and risk. \cite{ref310}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Seek reassessment if symptoms persist or worsen. A clinician may choose repeat testing or a different test.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-019.} \textbf{Myth:} A person with a concussion must be kept awake.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} After a minor head injury has been assessed and home observation is appropriate, sleep is generally allowed. Increasing drowsiness or difficulty waking is a warning sign and differs from ordinary sleep. \cite{ref405}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Follow the discharge instructions about observation. Seek emergency care for difficulty waking, repeated vomiting, a worsening headache, a seizure, or new weakness.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-020.} \textbf{Myth:} Sugar makes children hyperactive.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Blinded controlled studies have not found a consistent increase in children's activity or change in behavior after sugar consumption. Excitement at parties can coincide with sweets and make the association seem stronger. \cite{ref7}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Limit added sugar for established nutritional and dental reasons. Evaluate persistent attention or behavior concerns on their own merits.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-021.} \textbf{Myth:} Postpartum depression is rare.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Postpartum depression is common enough to merit routine attention. In a survey across participating US jurisdictions, about one in eight respondents reported postpartum depressive symptoms. A symptom survey does not establish a clinical diagnosis in every respondent. \cite{ref406}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Seek help for persistent low mood, loss of interest, or difficulty functioning after birth. Effective treatments are available.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-022.} \textbf{Myth:} Suicide affects only people with a mental illness.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Mental illness can increase suicide risk, but suicidal distress can occur without a known diagnosis. Acute loss, isolation, and other pressures can contribute, and no single factor explains every suicide. \cite{ref64}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Take expressions of suicidal intent seriously regardless of diagnostic history. If someone may act imminently, stay with them if safe and contact local emergency services.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-023.} \textbf{Myth:} Bleeding after menopause is normal.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Vaginal bleeding after menopause can come from thinning tissues, polyps, or other benign causes. It can also be an early sign of cancer of the uterine lining. \cite{ref65}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Arrange assessment even for one episode of spotting. Mention hormone treatment or other medicines when describing the bleeding.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-024.} \textbf{Myth:} Adults do not need routine vaccinations.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Vaccine protection and infection risks change over a lifetime. Adults may need boosters, missed childhood doses, or vaccines recommended for their age, pregnancy, medical condition, or exposure. \cite{ref66}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Review your vaccination record against the local adult schedule with a clinician or pharmacist.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-025.} \textbf{Myth:} Tanning beds are safer than outdoor sunlight.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Tanning beds expose skin to ultraviolet radiation, which damages skin and increases skin cancer risk. A controlled session or absence of a visible burn does not make the exposure safe. \cite{ref104,ref319}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Avoid indoor tanning. A cosmetic sunless tanner can change skin color but generally provides no sun protection on its own.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-026.} \textbf{Myth:} Oral or anal sex is automatically safe from sexually transmitted infections.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Sexually transmitted infections can spread through oral or anal sex. Risk varies with the organism, the body site, and the type of contact. An infection in the throat or rectum may cause no symptoms. \cite{ref407}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Use appropriate barriers, such as condoms or dental dams. Tell the clinician which sites were exposed so that testing can be tailored.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-027.} \textbf{Myth:} Blue light from ordinary screens causes permanent eye damage.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Evidence has not shown that blue light from ordinary screen use damages the retina. Prolonged near work and reduced blinking can cause temporary strain or dryness. \cite{ref408}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Take breaks, blink regularly, and adjust the display for comfort. Persistent blur or pain deserves assessment.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-028.} \textbf{Myth:} All heel pain is plantar fasciitis.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Heel pain can arise from the plantar fascia, Achilles tendon, heel pad, or a bone stress injury. The location and timing of pain help distinguish the possibilities. \cite{ref409}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Seek assessment for persistent pain or pain after a significant injury, especially if you cannot bear weight.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-029.} \textbf{Myth:} Heart failure means the heart has stopped beating.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} In heart failure, the heart still beats but has difficulty filling or pumping well enough to meet the body's needs. Cardiac arrest is a sudden loss of effective circulation and is a different emergency. \cite{ref46}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} New breathlessness or swelling merits assessment. If someone collapses and is unresponsive and not breathing normally, call emergency services, start CPR, and use an available AED.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-030.} \textbf{Myth:} Young people cannot develop heart disease.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Heart disease can be present from birth or develop in younger people through inherited conditions, inflammation, or early coronary disease. Risk generally increases with age. \cite{ref46}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Take exertional fainting or chest discomfort seriously at any age, particularly with a family history of sudden cardiac death.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-031.} \textbf{Myth:} A family history of heart disease means there is nothing you can do to prevent it.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} A family history can raise risk, particularly when a close relative developed heart disease early. Smoking, blood pressure, cholesterol, and other factors still influence individual risk. \cite{ref46}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Share the relative's diagnosis and age at onset with your clinician. This can guide earlier assessment and preventive treatment.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-032.} \textbf{Myth:} Cholesterol testing can wait until middle age.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} High cholesterol can be present well before middle age, especially in familial hypercholesterolemia. It usually causes no symptoms. \cite{ref410}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Ask when to begin testing based on age, family history, and local guidance. Early heart disease in a close relative is particularly relevant.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-033.} \textbf{Myth:} Leg pain is always ordinary aging and cannot be related to the heart or circulation.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Leg pain has several causes. Calf discomfort that repeatedly starts with walking and eases with rest can reflect reduced arterial blood flow, called peripheral artery disease. \cite{ref46}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Arrange assessment for this pattern. Sudden severe limb pain with coldness, pallor, or numbness needs emergency care.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-034.} \textbf{Myth:} A fast heartbeat automatically means a heart attack.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Heart rate can increase with exercise, fever, dehydration, anxiety, or an abnormal rhythm. Pulse rate alone cannot diagnose or exclude a heart attack. \cite{ref46}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Call emergency services for a racing heartbeat accompanied by chest discomfort, severe breathlessness, or fainting. Seek assessment for recurrent unexplained episodes.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-035.} \textbf{Myth:} Exercise should be avoided after a heart attack.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} After a heart attack, appropriately timed and supervised activity is an important part of recovery. The starting point depends on symptoms, treatment, and heart function. \cite{ref46}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Ask about cardiac rehabilitation and an individualized return to activity. New chest discomfort during recovery needs prompt attention.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-036.} \textbf{Myth:} Diabetes medication completely removes the cardiovascular risk caused by diabetes.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Some diabetes medicines lower cardiovascular risk, but none eliminates it. Blood pressure, cholesterol, smoking, kidney health, and other factors also affect outcomes. \cite{ref46,ref271}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Review cardiovascular risk with the care team alongside glucose control.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-037.} \textbf{Myth:} Oil pulling reverses cavities or cures gum disease.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Evidence does not establish that swishing oil repairs a cavity or cures gum disease. A cavity is a structural defect, and persistent gum inflammation can require professional treatment. \cite{ref50,ref45}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Brush with fluoride toothpaste, clean between teeth, and seek dental assessment for pain, a visible cavity, or persistent gum bleeding.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-038.} \textbf{Myth:} Charcoal toothpaste safely whitens teeth without harming them.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
