\textbf{Fact:} Evidence for reliable whitening with charcoal products is limited. Abrasiveness varies by formulation, and removing surface stains is different from changing the underlying tooth color. \cite{ref411}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Avoid abrasive scrubbing. Ask a dentist about stain removal or whitening options that suit your enamel and restorations.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-039.} \textbf{Myth:} DIY braces, rubber bands, or home veneers are safe alternatives to dental treatment.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Unsupervised devices can move teeth in unintended directions or injure the gums and supporting tissues. Covering teeth with a cosmetic appliance can also conceal untreated decay. \cite{ref47,ref50}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Have the teeth, gums, and bite assessed before attempting to move or cover teeth.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-040.} \textbf{Myth:} Mouth taping is a safe treatment for snoring or sleep apnea.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Small studies have tested mouth taping in selected people, but evidence has not established it as a safe, effective general treatment for snoring or obstructive sleep apnea. Closing the mouth can impede breathing when the nose is obstructed. \cite{ref412,ref283}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Seek assessment for loud snoring with pauses in breathing, gasping, or daytime sleepiness. Do not substitute mouth tape for prescribed treatment.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-041.} \textbf{Myth:} ``Quick and easy'' weight-loss products are reliably safe and effective.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Rapid weight-loss claims are often unsupported. Some products sold as supplements have contained undeclared drugs, so a label alone cannot establish either effectiveness or safety. \cite{ref48,ref121}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Check the evidence for the specific product and seek clinical advice before using a product promising rapid loss without sustained changes.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-042.} \textbf{Myth:} Unapproved home tests can reliably diagnose serious disease.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} The accuracy of a home test depends on validation for the condition, the sample, the timing, and correct use. A sales claim cannot establish how often it misses disease or gives a false alarm. \cite{ref413}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Choose a test authorized for its intended use where you live. Follow its instructions on confirmation and seek care for concerning symptoms regardless of the result.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-043.} \textbf{Myth:} Unapproved supplements are safer than prescription treatment because they are natural.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Natural origin does not establish a product's dose, purity, effectiveness, or safety. Supplements can interact with medicines, and some products marketed for medical conditions contain undeclared ingredients. \cite{ref48,ref117}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Tell a pharmacist or clinician exactly what you take before substituting a supplement for prescribed treatment.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-044.} \textbf{Myth:} Anti-aging pills can reliably stop or reverse normal aging.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} No pill has been shown to reliably stop or reverse normal human aging. Correcting a deficiency or changing a laboratory marker is a more limited outcome. \cite{ref266}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Ask whether a study measured a meaningful health benefit in humans and what adverse effects occurred.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-045.} \textbf{Myth:} Taking cholesterol medicine means diet and lifestyle changes are no longer needed.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Cholesterol-lowering medicine can substantially reduce LDL cholesterol and cardiovascular risk. Diet, activity, and avoiding tobacco can provide additional benefits, including effects on blood pressure and fitness. \cite{ref46}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Continue the agreed medication and prevention plan. A lower cholesterol reading is a reason to review progress rather than assume other measures no longer matter.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-046.} \textbf{Myth:} A food label showing ``no cholesterol'' means the food is heart-healthy.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} A cholesterol-free food can still contain substantial saturated fat, sodium, or added sugar. The claim describes one component of the food. \cite{ref10}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Read the nutrition information for the portion you actually eat, including saturated fat and sodium.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-047.} \textbf{Myth:} Switching from butter to any margarine automatically lowers cholesterol.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Replacing butter with a spread richer in unsaturated fat and lower in saturated fat can help lower LDL cholesterol. Margarines differ in their fat composition. \cite{ref10}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Compare saturated fat per serving and choose a spread based on unsaturated oils. Check for partially hydrogenated oils where they are still sold.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-048.} \textbf{Myth:} Children do not need to worry about cholesterol.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Children can have elevated LDL cholesterol, particularly with inherited familial hypercholesterolemia. The condition often produces no visible signs. \cite{ref410}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Tell the child's clinician about a parent with very high cholesterol or a close relative with early heart disease. Testing recommendations depend on risk and local guidance.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-049.} \textbf{Myth:} Sexual-enhancement supplements sold as natural products are reliably safe.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Some products marketed for sexual enhancement have contained undeclared prescription-drug ingredients. These can interact dangerously with nitrate medicines used for angina. \cite{ref414}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Discuss erectile symptoms or a proposed supplement with a clinician or pharmacist. A natural label cannot rule out a drug interaction.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-050.} \textbf{Myth:} Bodybuilding and sports-performance supplements are safe if they are sold as dietary supplements.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Sports supplements vary widely. Some bodybuilding products have contained undeclared steroid-like drugs and have been linked to serious adverse effects, including liver injury. \cite{ref415}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Review the exact product and ingredients with a qualified professional. A supplement label alone is no assurance of safety.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-051.} \textbf{Myth:} Chewing special ``fitness gum'' safely sculpts the jawline.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} A randomized chewing-gum training study found increased bite force without a significant change in mandibular shape. It does not establish that hard fitness gum produces the advertised cosmetic effect. \cite{ref416}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Avoid prolonged forceful chewing if it causes jaw pain or aggravates an existing jaw problem.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-052.} \textbf{Myth:} Lemon juice is a safe natural teeth-whitening treatment.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Lemon juice is acidic and can erode tooth enamel. Repeated acid exposure can make teeth sensitive and expose more of the yellow dentin underneath. \cite{ref411}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Avoid using lemon as a bleaching treatment. Discuss persistent stains or a single discolored tooth with a dentist.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-053.} \textbf{Myth:} Swishing concentrated hydrogen peroxide is a safe way to whiten teeth at home.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Peroxide can bleach teeth in appropriately formulated products. A concentrated solution used as a mouth rinse can burn oral tissues and cause sensitivity. \cite{ref411,ref50}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Use dental whitening products only as directed. Seek advice before whitening if you have pain, gum disease, or damaged teeth.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-054.} \textbf{Myth:} Sparkling water is completely harmless to teeth.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Plain unsweetened sparkling water is generally a tooth-friendly alternative to soda. Citrus flavoring, added acids or sugar, and repeated sipping can change the risk of enamel erosion or decay. \cite{ref417}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Check the ingredients. Have acidic flavored drinks with a meal rather than sipping them frequently over the day.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-055.} \textbf{Myth:} ``Natural'' whitening or filing is a safe substitute for dental treatment.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Acidic or abrasive home whitening methods can remove enamel. Filing changes a tooth's shape irreversibly and can expose sensitive tissue. \cite{ref411,ref50}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Have sharp edges, uneven teeth, or discoloration assessed by a dentist before removing any tooth material.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-056.} \textbf{Myth:} 5G mobile networks cause COVID-19.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} COVID-19 is caused by infection with SARS-CoV-2. Radio signals from mobile networks cannot transmit a respiratory virus. Transmission also occurs in places without 5G coverage. \cite{ref51}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Use infection prevention measures that address respiratory spread, including ventilation in shared indoor spaces.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-057.} \textbf{Myth:} Hot weather, sunlight, or high temperatures prevent COVID-19.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} COVID-19 can spread in hot climates and in all seasons. Outdoor air movement can reduce exposure compared with crowded indoor settings, but heat or sunlight does not guarantee protection. \cite{ref51}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Base precautions on exposure and ventilation. Deliberate overheating or prolonged sun exposure has no established preventive benefit.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-058.} \textbf{Myth:} Eating garlic prevents COVID-19.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Garlic can be part of a normal diet. Eating it has not been shown to prevent SARS-CoV-2 infection in people. \cite{ref51}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Do not rely on a food or supplement in place of evidence-based prevention or timely medical care.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-059.} \textbf{Myth:} Drinking alcohol prevents or cures COVID-19.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Drinking alcohol does not prevent or treat COVID-19. The use of alcohol-based products on surfaces or hands does not make alcohol an internal disinfectant. \cite{ref51}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Never ingest hand sanitizer or surface disinfectants. Seek poison advice promptly if one is swallowed.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-060.} \textbf{Myth:} Hydroxychloroquine or chloroquine reliably prevents or cures COVID-19.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Randomized clinical trials did not establish a preventive or treatment benefit for hydroxychloroquine in COVID-19. WHO recommends against using it for these purposes. Chloroquine is likewise not an established COVID-19 treatment, and both medicines can cause serious adverse effects. \cite{ref418,ref51}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Do not self-medicate for COVID-19 with these drugs. If prescribed one for another condition, discuss any change with the prescriber.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-061.} \textbf{Myth:} Antibiotics prevent or cure COVID-19.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} COVID-19 is caused by a virus. Antibiotics can treat a separate bacterial infection when clinically indicated, but they do not prevent or cure the viral infection. \cite{ref51,ref391}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} If you are at higher risk for severe COVID-19, contact a clinician early about antiviral treatment eligibility.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-062.} \textbf{Myth:} UV lamps safely disinfect a person's hands or skin.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Germicidal UV lamps designed for air or surfaces can injure skin and eyes. Safety depends on wavelength, dose, and device design. An ordinary germicidal lamp is unsuitable for disinfecting hands. \cite{ref51,ref315}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Wash hands with soap and water or use an appropriate alcohol-based hand sanitizer. Follow device instructions that prevent exposure to people.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-063.} \textbf{Myth:} A thermal scanner can diagnose COVID-19.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} A thermal scanner estimates skin temperature. Fever has many causes, and people with COVID-19 can have a normal temperature. A scan alone can neither confirm nor exclude infection. \cite{ref51}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} If symptoms or exposure make infection a concern, follow appropriate testing guidance even after a normal temperature check.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-064.} \textbf{Myth:} Daily aspirin is safe and beneficial for every adult to prevent a first heart attack or stroke.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} For someone who has never had a heart attack or stroke, the possible benefit of daily aspirin must be weighed against bleeding risk. Age, cardiovascular risk, and bleeding history influence that balance. \cite{ref52}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Ask before starting aspirin for prevention. If aspirin was prescribed after a heart attack or stroke, discuss changes with the prescriber because that is a different indication.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-065.} \textbf{Myth:} Statins are usually dangerous and their side effects outweigh their cardiovascular benefits.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} When a statin is appropriately prescribed, its expected cardiovascular benefit generally outweighs its risks. Muscle symptoms can occur, serious muscle injury is uncommon, and the absolute benefit varies with baseline risk. \cite{ref53}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Report new symptoms. A clinician can review possible causes and discuss a different dose or statin if needed.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-066.} \textbf{Myth:} Mercury or thimerosal in vaccines causes autism.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Studies comparing vaccinated children have not found evidence that thimerosal causes autism. Thimerosal is an ethylmercury-containing preservative used in some multidose vaccine vials. \cite{ref2,ref54}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} A question about a particular formulation can be answered from its ingredient list. The autism claim is not a reason to delay vaccination.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-067.} \textbf{Myth:} Properly worn medical masks cause oxygen deficiency or carbon-dioxide poisoning.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} A medical mask allows oxygen and carbon dioxide to pass through. Studies of routine surgical mask use have not shown clinically important gas-exchange problems in most wearers. Warmth or discomfort alone does not indicate poisoning. \cite{ref419,ref51}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Check the fit if a mask is uncomfortable. New or severe breathlessness deserves assessment regardless of whether a mask is being worn.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-068.} \textbf{Myth:} Vitamin D, vitamin C, or zinc supplements cure COVID-19.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Clinical evidence has not established vitamin D, vitamin C, or zinc supplements as cures for COVID-19. Treating a documented deficiency is a separate medical indication. \cite{ref420,ref391}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} If you are at higher risk for severe illness, seek timely advice about antiviral treatment. Relying on supplements can use up the short window for starting it.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-069.} \textbf{Myth:} Swimming pools or ordinary water transmit COVID-19 between swimmers.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} COVID-19 spreads primarily through infectious particles in the air. Evidence does not identify swimming-pool water as a route between swimmers. People in a crowded indoor pool facility can still share air. \cite{ref421}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Consider ventilation and crowding in changing rooms and other indoor areas. Pool disinfection does not address exposure through shared air.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-070.} \textbf{Myth:} MSG universally causes headaches and other symptoms.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Controlled studies have not consistently reproduced symptoms attributed to monosodium glutamate (MSG) under ordinary eating conditions. Some people report short-lived symptoms after unusually large amounts taken without food. \cite{ref55}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} If symptoms recur, record the amount, meal, and timing. A reaction reported by one person does not establish a universal effect.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-071.} \textbf{Myth:} Irradiated food becomes radioactive.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Approved food irradiation uses controlled ionizing radiation to reduce microbes or pests. This exposure does not make the food radioactive. \cite{ref56}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Handle and store irradiated food as directed. Irradiation reduces certain risks but does not prevent later contamination.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-072.} \textbf{Myth:} All genetically modified foods are inherently unsafe to eat.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Genetic modification describes a method used to introduce a trait. Food safety depends on the particular product, including its composition and potential allergens. The method alone does not establish that a food is unsafe. \cite{ref57}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Evaluate a concern about a specific food or trait against the evidence for that product.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-073.} \textbf{Myth:} Long COVID is imaginary, purely psychological, or always permanent.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Long COVID is a recognized condition that can affect several organ systems. Symptoms may persist, fluctuate, improve, or recur. Routine test results can be normal, and recovery varies between people. \cite{ref422}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Seek assessment for persistent symptoms affecting daily life. A clinician can consider alternative causes and tailor symptom management to your limitations.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-074.} \textbf{Myth:} Eating eggs causes heart disease.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Egg yolks contain dietary cholesterol, but eating eggs does not by itself determine whether someone develops heart disease. The overall dietary pattern, including saturated fat intake, influences cardiovascular risk. \cite{ref10}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Consider what usually accompanies or replaces the eggs. Individual advice may differ for someone trying to lower LDL cholesterol.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-075.} \textbf{Myth:} Everyone needs a daily multivitamin to stay healthy.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Most people can obtain many of their nutrient requirements from food. A supplement can be appropriate for a specific need, such as folic acid before pregnancy or vitamin B12 when intake or absorption is inadequate. \cite{ref20}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Choose a supplement for a defined reason and check for overlapping ingredients if taking several products.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-076.} \textbf{Myth:} Breakfast is essential for weight loss, and skipping it automatically causes weight gain.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Randomized trials do not show that adding breakfast is a reliable weight-loss strategy for everyone. The studies were generally short, so they also cannot settle every question about long-term meal patterns. \cite{ref423}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Choose a meal pattern that suits your appetite, schedule, and medical needs. Overall intake matters more than treating breakfast as compulsory.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-077.} \textbf{Myth:} Only alcohol causes liver disease.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Alcohol is one cause of liver disease. Other causes include metabolic dysfunction-associated steatotic liver disease, viral hepatitis, autoimmune disease, and certain medicines. \cite{ref424}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Report relevant medical history, medicines, supplements, and alcohol use when a liver problem is being investigated.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-078.} \textbf{Myth:} If you have no symptoms, you cannot have liver disease.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Liver disease can progress for years with few or no symptoms. Some people first learn of it through risk-based testing or abnormal blood or imaging results. \cite{ref424}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Discuss testing if you have risk factors, even without pain or jaundice.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-079.} \textbf{Myth:} Liver damage is always permanent and cannot improve.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Some forms of liver injury can improve when the cause is treated. Fat accumulation and inflammation may improve, and some scarring can regress. Advanced cirrhosis can leave lasting damage and ongoing risks. \cite{ref424,ref21}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Treating the cause remains worthwhile even after scarring develops. Continue recommended monitoring if cirrhosis has been diagnosed.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-080.} \textbf{Myth:} All hepatitis is caused by a virus.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Hepatitis means inflammation of the liver. Viruses are important causes, but autoimmune disease, alcohol, and certain medicines can also cause it. \cite{ref425,ref424}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Treatment and the possibility of transmission depend on the cause. The word hepatitis alone does not mean a person is infectious.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-081.} \textbf{Myth:} A daily glass of red wine is good for the heart and is harmless.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Observational reports linking modest wine intake with better heart outcomes cannot establish that wine caused the difference. Alcohol also carries risks, including high blood pressure and several cancers. \cite{ref426}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} There is no need to start drinking for heart protection. Discuss alcohol use if you have high blood pressure, liver disease, or medicines that interact with it.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-082.} \textbf{Myth:} Persistent pain is only in a person's head.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Persistent pain is a real experience influenced by biological, psychological, and social factors. Changes in the nervous system can sustain pain after an injury heals or when imaging does not show an obvious cause. \cite{ref234}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} A pain assessment should address daily function, sleep, and possible causes. Psychological and physical treatments can both be useful.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-083.} \textbf{Myth:} Pain medication will fix the underlying problem by itself.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Pain medicines can relieve symptoms, and some reduce inflammation. Relief does not by itself establish that the underlying injury or illness has resolved. \cite{ref234,ref250}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Review persistent or worsening pain and discuss whether treatment should also address an underlying cause or restore function.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-084.} \textbf{Myth:} Doctors will not work as hard to save a patient who is a registered organ donor.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} The treating team's first responsibility is to save the patient's life. In deceased organ donation, decisions about the patient's care and the determination of death are separate from organ recovery. \cite{ref427}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} You can document donation preferences without changing the emergency treatment you should receive.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-085.} \textbf{Myth:} Older adults or people with medical conditions cannot donate organs.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} There is no universal upper age limit for deceased organ donation. Suitability depends on the organ, the person's condition, and transplant program criteria. Some conditions rule out particular donations. \cite{ref427}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} If you wish to donate, check your local registration process and let the transplant team assess suitability when appropriate.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-086.} \textbf{Myth:} A donor's family must pay the medical costs of organ donation.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} In the United States, a deceased donor's family is not billed for organ recovery and other donation-related medical costs. Charges for care before death and funeral expenses are separate. Arrangements depend on the jurisdiction. \cite{ref427}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Ask the local donation organization which costs are covered if the distinction is unclear.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-087.} \textbf{Myth:} Duct tape is a proven and reliable cure for warts.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Trials of duct tape for common skin warts have produced inconsistent results. Some warts disappear without treatment, which can make a remedy seem effective. \cite{ref284}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} For a troublesome wart, discuss treatments such as salicylic acid or clinician-applied freezing. Get advice first for lesions on the face or genitals or if the diagnosis is uncertain.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-088.} \textbf{Myth:} Coffee, greasy food, more alcohol, or a supplement can reliably cure a hangover.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Time is the main factor in recovery from a hangover. Fluids or food may relieve particular symptoms, but coffee, supplements, and additional alcohol do not reliably end a hangover or restore safe driving ability. \cite{ref27}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Avoid driving while impaired. Someone who is hard to wake or breathing abnormally after drinking needs emergency care.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-089.} \textbf{Myth:} All artificial sweeteners are poisonous and cause cancer.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Sweeteners differ chemically and are assessed individually. Regulatory intake limits reflect evidence about dose and exposure. A possible cancer hazard identified for one compound does not establish that all sweeteners cause cancer in ordinary use. \cite{ref28}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Consider the specific ingredient and amount. People with phenylketonuria need to avoid or restrict aspartame because it supplies phenylalanine.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-090.} \textbf{Myth:} Talcum powder definitely causes cancer in everyone who uses it.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} IARC classified talc as probably carcinogenic to humans in 2024. That classification describes a hazard; it does not predict that every user will develop cancer or quantify the risk from a particular use. Talc contaminated with asbestos poses an additional established hazard. \cite{ref428,ref285}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Distinguish the product, route, and extent of exposure when interpreting a cancer claim about talc.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-091.} \textbf{Myth:} Hormonal contraception inevitably causes depression or is unsafe for anyone with a history of depression.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} A history of depression alone generally does not preclude hormonal contraception. Mood changes can occur, and individual experiences vary. Some medicines also affect contraceptive choices. \cite{ref429}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Discuss persistent mood changes or medication interactions with the prescriber so that alternatives and continued pregnancy prevention can be considered.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-092.} \textbf{Myth:} Suicide is contagious like an infectious disease.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Suicide is not transmitted by a pathogen. In suicide prevention, contagion refers to an increased risk of suicidal behavior in some people after exposure to a suicide or certain kinds of reporting. This effect is neither inevitable nor a reason to avoid supportive conversation. \cite{ref430}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Offer support after a suicide, use sensitive reporting, and take expressions of immediate intent seriously. Seek emergency help if someone is in immediate danger.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-093.} \textbf{Myth:} Testosterone supplements reliably increase libido, energy, and health in every man.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Prescription testosterone can help some men with symptoms and consistently low testosterone confirmed by appropriate testing. It is not a general energy or libido enhancer, and products sold as testosterone boosters differ from prescribed hormone treatment. \cite{ref431}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Discuss persistent symptoms and fertility plans before starting treatment. Testosterone therapy can suppress sperm production and requires follow-up.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-094.} \textbf{Myth:} Alcohol helps you get good-quality sleep.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Alcohol may make falling asleep easier at first, but it can disrupt sleep later in the night and worsen snoring or sleep apnea. \cite{ref432}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} If you wake repeatedly after drinking, consider the timing and amount of alcohol rather than using another drink as a sleep aid.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-095.} \textbf{Myth:} Older people need almost no sleep.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Older adults still need substantial sleep. Sleep may become lighter or shift earlier, but needing almost none is not a normal consequence of aging. \cite{ref385}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Persistent daytime sleepiness, loud snoring, or repeated waking merits assessment rather than being dismissed as age-related.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-096.} \textbf{Myth:} You can train yourself to function normally on very little sleep.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Repeated sleep restriction can impair attention and reaction time even when someone feels accustomed to short nights. Individual sleep needs vary, but feeling adapted does not establish normal performance. \cite{ref432}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Allow a regular opportunity for adequate sleep, especially before driving or safety-sensitive work.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-097.} \textbf{Myth:} Sleeping pills permanently cure insomnia.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Sleep medicines can help selected people, but they do not reliably cure chronic insomnia permanently. Cognitive behavioral therapy for insomnia addresses patterns that sustain it and is usually the first treatment for chronic insomnia. \cite{ref433}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Discuss ongoing insomnia and the benefits and risks of medication. Ask before stopping a regularly used sleep medicine abruptly.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-098.} \textbf{Myth:} Insulin is only used when diabetes becomes extremely severe.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Insulin is essential in type 1 diabetes and may be used at different stages of type 2 diabetes. It can also be needed temporarily during pregnancy, illness, or other changes in glucose control. \cite{ref260,ref271}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Ask why insulin is recommended in your situation and how to use it safely. Starting it is not a measure of personal failure.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-099.} \textbf{Myth:} Vitamins provide energy like calories do.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Vitamins do not provide calories. Several B vitamins help the body use energy from carbohydrate, fat, and protein. Correcting a deficiency can improve fatigue without the vitamin itself acting as fuel. \cite{ref434}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Persistent fatigue deserves assessment of its cause. Extra vitamins do not substitute for adequate food or sleep.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-100.} \textbf{Myth:} X-rays remain inside your body after an examination.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} During an ordinary X-ray, radiation briefly passes through the body or is absorbed. The beam stops when the equipment switches off, and the examination does not make the person radioactive. \cite{ref435}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} No isolation is needed after a plain X-ray. Nuclear medicine tests use radioactive tracers and may have different instructions.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-101.} \textbf{Myth:} MRI uses ionizing radiation like an X-ray.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} MRI uses a strong magnetic field and radiofrequency energy to form images. It does not use ionizing X-rays. The magnetic field does create important safety considerations for certain implants and metal objects. \cite{ref435,ref436}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Tell the imaging team about implants, devices, or possible metal fragments before entering the scanner area.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-102.} \textbf{Myth:} Ultrasound uses radiation like an X-ray.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Ultrasound forms images using high-frequency sound waves and returning echoes. It does not give the patient an ionizing radiation dose. \cite{ref435}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} The choice between ultrasound and another test depends on the clinical question and what structures need to be seen.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-103.} \textbf{Myth:} Every headache indicates a brain tumor.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Common causes of headache include migraine and tension-type headache. A headache alone cannot diagnose a brain tumor. The pattern, onset, examination, and accompanying symptoms guide assessment. \cite{ref437}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Seek emergency care for a sudden severe headache that reaches maximum intensity rapidly, or a headache with new weakness, confusion, or trouble speaking.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-104.} \textbf{Myth:} Stroke always causes pain.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} A stroke can cause sudden loss of strength, speech, vision, balance, or coordination without pain. Some strokes do cause a severe headache. \cite{ref438}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Call emergency services immediately for sudden stroke symptoms, even if they resolve. Note when the person was last known to be well.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-105.} \textbf{Myth:} Stroke happens only to elderly people.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Stroke risk increases with age, but stroke can occur in younger adults and children. Age alone cannot exclude it. \cite{ref438}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Treat sudden facial droop, arm weakness, or speech difficulty as an emergency at any age.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-106.} \textbf{Myth:} Panic attacks always indicate heart disease.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Panic attacks can cause palpitations, chest discomfort, and breathlessness. Similar symptoms can arise from heart or lung disease, so the sensation alone cannot distinguish them. \cite{ref232}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Seek emergency assessment for new or severe chest symptoms, especially with fainting or marked breathlessness. Recurrent panic attacks also have effective treatments.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-107.} \textbf{Myth:} Arthritis affects only old people.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Arthritis includes several conditions. Osteoarthritis is more frequent with age, while inflammatory arthritis can begin in childhood or young adulthood. \cite{ref439}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Persistent joint swelling, stiffness, or a limp in a child or young adult warrants assessment.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-108.} \textbf{Myth:} Pain always means tissue damage is occurring.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Pain can accompany actual or threatened injury. It can also persist because the nervous system has become more sensitive. Its intensity is not a direct measurement of tissue damage. \cite{ref234}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Use changes in symptoms, function, examination, and injury history to guide activity and treatment.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-109.} \textbf{Myth:} No pain means there is no serious disease.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Some serious conditions, including high blood pressure and early kidney disease, often cause no pain. Pain is only one possible warning sign. \cite{ref267,ref272}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Follow risk-based screening and seek assessment for other concerning changes, even when they do not hurt.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-110.} \textbf{Myth:} Tooth loss is an inevitable part of aging.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Decay and gum disease can lead to tooth loss, but many people retain natural teeth throughout life. Age alone does not make teeth fall out. \cite{ref235}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Use fluoride toothpaste, clean between teeth, and seek dental care for loose teeth, pain, or ongoing gum bleeding.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-111.} \textbf{Myth:} Bleeding gums are normal.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Gums that bleed repeatedly during brushing or cleaning between teeth may be inflamed from plaque. Injury, medications, and other conditions can also contribute. \cite{ref236}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Clean gently and arrange a dental assessment for recurring bleeding. Mention medicines that affect bleeding, and do not stop them without medical advice.
\item[] \textbf{IMP-112.} \textbf{Myth:} All skin rashes are contagious.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} A rash describes a skin change, not a single diagnosis. Eczema and contact dermatitis are not contagious. Some infections, such as ringworm, can spread between people. \cite{ref440}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Advice about contact or treatment depends on the cause. Seek urgent help for a rash with difficulty breathing or swelling of the lips or tongue.
\end{enumerate}

\input{indian_myths}

\chapter{Marketing-Created Myths}
Some health myths were not inherited from folklore but manufactured by advertising. This chapter documents claims that were created, amplified, or sustained primarily by commercial marketing, together with what regulators, courts, or published research found. The examples draw on public records, including Federal Trade Commission actions, Food and Drug Administration advisories, court rulings, and peer-reviewed historical analyses. Naming a company reflects the documentary record and is not commentary on any organization's current products. Marketing can spread misinformation without breaking any law, and several campaigns described here predate modern advertising standards. Recognizing the commercial origin of a belief is often the first step toward correcting it.
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=0pt,labelwidth=0pt,labelsep=0pt,itemindent=0pt]

\item[] \textbf{MKT-001.} \textbf{Myth:} Adhesive foot pads pull toxins, heavy metals, and parasites out of the body through the soles of the feet overnight.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} A pad darkening overnight does not identify any toxin or show that it left the body. The US Federal Trade Commission challenged these claims for Kinoki pads, and a court barred the marketers from selling them. \cite{ref83,ref84}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Suspected lead exposure or a parasitic infection calls for an exposure history and appropriate testing, not inspection of a used foot pad.
\item[] \textbf{MKT-002.} \textbf{Myth:} An effervescent cold remedy invented by a schoolteacher prevents colds.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Airborne's inventor story did not establish that the product prevents colds. The FTC challenged the prevention claim because the company lacked adequate evidence; the settlement was not a clinical trial of the product. \cite{ref464}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Judge a prevention claim by studies measuring actual colds in comparable groups. A list of vitamins alone cannot establish the benefit.
\item[] \textbf{MKT-003.} \textbf{Myth:} A daily antiseptic mouthwash can replace flossing and prevent colds and sore throats.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Some therapeutic mouthwashes can help reduce plaque or gingivitis as an addition to brushing and cleaning between teeth. A rinse cannot reliably replace interdental cleaning, and routine use has not been established to prevent colds or sore throats. \cite{ref86,ref44}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Choose a rinse for a specific dental purpose. Continue brushing with fluoride toothpaste and cleaning between teeth.
\item[] \textbf{MKT-004.} \textbf{Myth:} A sugary breakfast cereal improves children's attentiveness by nearly 20 percent compared with other mornings.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} The Frosted Mini-Wheats advertisement emphasized a nearly 20 percent improvement. The study compared the cereal with no breakfast, and the average advantage in attentiveness was under 11 percent. It did not establish superiority over other breakfasts. \cite{ref87}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} When an advertisement reports a percentage, check what the comparison group ate and whether the figure describes the average child or a subgroup.
\item[] \textbf{MKT-005.} \textbf{Myth:} A cocoa breakfast cereal supports the immune system during flu season.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Kellogg marketed cereals with an immunity claim during flu season. Adding vitamins needed for normal immune function does not demonstrate that the finished cereal prevents infection or reduces illness in children. \cite{ref465}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Evaluate the cereal's overall nutrition. A package claim about immunity cannot substitute for evidence of fewer or less severe infections.
\item[] \textbf{MKT-006.} \textbf{Myth:} Pomegranate juice slows prostate cancer and treats erectile dysfunction.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} The studies used to market POM products did not substantiate claims that the juice treats prostate cancer or erectile dysfunction. A laboratory finding or change in a biomarker is different from demonstrated improvement in survival, symptoms, or sexual function. \cite{ref89}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Pomegranate juice can be consumed as a food. Seek medical assessment for erectile dysfunction or cancer rather than relying on the advertised treatment effect.
\item[] \textbf{MKT-007.} \textbf{Myth:} A probiotic yogurt with special bacterial strains relieves digestive irregularity better than ordinary yogurt.\newline
\textbf{Status:} Uncertain.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Results for a probiotic apply to the specific strains, dose, duration, and outcome studied. The FTC challenged claims that one daily serving of Activia was clinically proven to relieve occasional irregularity. That finding does not settle every comparison between probiotic and ordinary yogurt. \cite{ref466}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Check the serving regimen and comparator before applying a study result. Persistent changes in bowel habits deserve assessment.
\item[] \textbf{MKT-008.} \textbf{Myth:} Bracelets with hologram chips or ion technology improve balance, strength, flexibility, or pain.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Controlled testing has not established that a wristband's hologram improves balance, strength, or flexibility, or that an ionized bracelet relieves pain through an ion effect. Repeating a balance demonstration can change performance even without a bracelet. \cite{ref92,ref93}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} A fair comparison uses an indistinguishable control and a tester who does not know which bracelet is being worn.
\item[] \textbf{MKT-009.} \textbf{Myth:} Curved-sole toning shoes sculpt legs and buttocks during ordinary walking.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} A curved sole can change how walking feels. That mechanical difference does not establish that wearing the shoe produces the advertised changes in muscle tone or body shape. US regulators challenged toning claims made for specific brands. \cite{ref94,ref95}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Choose footwear for fit, comfort, and the activity. An unstable-feeling shoe may be unsuitable for someone with balance difficulties.
\item[] \textbf{MKT-010.} \textbf{Myth:} Intranasal zinc cold gels and swabs are proven, safe ways to shorten colds.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} The FDA warned about loss of smell, sometimes lasting, after use of certain zinc-containing nasal gels and swabs. Evidence about an oral zinc lozenge does not establish the safety or benefit of putting zinc in the nose. \cite{ref96,ref195}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Avoid intranasal zinc cold remedies. Check the ingredients and route, since products sold under the same brand can differ.
\item[] \textbf{MKT-011.} \textbf{Myth:} Essential oils can treat cancer, autism, and serious infections.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Clinical evidence has not established essential oils as treatments for cancer, autism, or serious infections. A scent used for comfort is a different intervention from a medicine shown to alter a disease course. \cite{ref97,ref453}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Do not delay clinical care for a serious infection or cancer. Ask about safety before applying a concentrated oil or taking any oil by mouth.
\item[] \textbf{MKT-012.} \textbf{Myth:} Tiny homeopathic pellets prevent influenza.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Trials of the homeopathic product Oscillococcinum have not established that it prevents influenza. Evidence about one product should be described in terms of the outcomes actually measured. \cite{ref467,ref40}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Use influenza vaccination when recommended. Someone at high risk of severe flu should seek prompt advice about antiviral treatment if symptoms develop.
\item[] \textbf{MKT-013.} \textbf{Myth:} Doctors endorsed a cigarette brand as their own favorite, proving it was the sensible choice.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} The Camel campaign used claims about doctors' brand preferences to imply reassurance. Even a representative survey of preferences could not establish that one cigarette protects smokers from disease. \cite{ref100,ref101}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Look for evidence on illness and mortality when comparing health risks. A testimonial says little about either outcome.
\item[] \textbf{MKT-014.} \textbf{Myth:} Light, mild, and low-tar cigarettes are a safer alternative for smokers.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Light and low-tar labels were based on machine measurements. How a person smokes determines actual exposure, and lower machine yields did not translate into a safer cigarette. \cite{ref101,ref102}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Switching to a light brand is not a reliable way to reduce smoking-related disease. Stopping smoking reduces risk.
\item[] \textbf{MKT-015.} \textbf{Myth:} Cigarettes marketed as additive-free or natural are less harmful.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Burning tobacco produces harmful substances even when no additives are used. A claim about ingredients does not establish a reduction in the risk of cancer, heart disease, or lung disease. \cite{ref103,ref101}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Treat natural and additive-free as descriptions of marketing or formulation, not as evidence of safety.
\item[] \textbf{MKT-016.} \textbf{Myth:} A base tan protects the skin, and indoor tanning is a safe, necessary source of vitamin D.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} A base tan offers little protection against further ultraviolet injury. Indoor tanning exposes skin to carcinogenic UV radiation. Vitamin D needs can be addressed without using a tanning bed. \cite{ref104,ref105}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Use shade, protective clothing, and sunscreen to limit UV injury. Seek dietary or supplement advice if vitamin D is a concern.
\item[] \textbf{MKT-017.} \textbf{Myth:} Dietary fat is the principal dietary cause of heart disease, so sugary foods are heart-safe.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Heart risk depends partly on the type of fat and what replaces it. Replacing saturated fat with unsaturated fat can lower LDL cholesterol. Replacing it with sugary food is a different dietary change. Historical industry documents also show how research priorities shaped the public debate. \cite{ref10,ref107,ref375}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Judge a dietary substitution by both foods involved. A low-fat label alone does not make a high-sugar food heart-healthy.
\item[] \textbf{MKT-018.} \textbf{Myth:} The hearty bacon-and-eggs breakfast is the scientifically ideal way to start the day.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} The bacon-and-eggs campaign drew on doctors' opinions about a hearty breakfast. It did not compare health outcomes across different breakfasts. \cite{ref108,ref375}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Choose a breakfast that fits the overall diet. Eggs and processed meats have different nutritional profiles.
\item[] \textbf{MKT-019.} \textbf{Myth:} A glass of fruit juice at breakfast is nutritionally equivalent to eating fruit.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Juice usually supplies less fiber than whole fruit, and several pieces of fruit can fit into one glass. \cite{ref109}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Choose whole fruit regularly and include juice portions when planning carbohydrate intake.
\item[] \textbf{MKT-020.} \textbf{Myth:} Vitamin-fortified sweetened waters are a healthy way to meet daily nutrient needs.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Fortification supplies the vitamins on the label. Sweetened water can also add substantial sugar, and a few vitamins cannot meet all dietary needs. \cite{ref106,ref20,ref375}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Compare the serving size, added sugar, and nutrients with your needs.
\item[] \textbf{MKT-021.} \textbf{Myth:} Antibacterial soaps protect households better than plain soap and water.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Consumer antibacterial washes have not been shown to prevent more illness in households than plain soap and water. \cite{ref394}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Wash thoroughly before preparing food and after using the toilet. Clinical antisepsis is a separate use.
\item[] \textbf{MKT-022.} \textbf{Myth:} Alkaline or ionized water neutralizes body acidity and prevents disease.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} The lungs and kidneys regulate blood acidity. A review found no evidence that drinking alkaline water prevents or treats cancer. \cite{ref111}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Choose safe drinking water. Suspected disturbances of blood acidity require medical assessment.
\item[] \textbf{MKT-023.} \textbf{Myth:} Cellulite is a disease that special creams can eliminate.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Cellulite is common dimpling related to fat and connective tissue. Some products temporarily alter its appearance, but lasting elimination by a cream has not been established. \cite{ref112}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Cellulite itself requires no treatment. Ask how long any cosmetic change lasts.
\item[] \textbf{MKT-024.} \textbf{Myth:} Collagen creams rebuild youthful skin by restoring collagen from a jar.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Moisturizers can temporarily soften fine lines. Listing collagen as an ingredient does not establish replacement of collagen deep in the skin. \cite{ref468}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Look for evidence on the specific formulation and the skin change it claims to produce.

\end{enumerate}

\chapter{Alternative Medicine Myths}
Traditional systems such as homeopathy, Ayurveda, and traditional Chinese medicine carry deep cultural history, and many practices offer comfort, community, and physical activity. This chapter evaluates specific health claims against clinical evidence and regulatory findings; it does not dismiss the cultures from which these practices come. Three recurring problems appear across the examples. First, plausible traditions can still rest on incorrect biology. Second, natural origin does not guarantee safety, because some preparations contain potent pharmacological agents, toxic metals, or undeclared drugs. Third, the greatest harm usually comes not from the practice itself but from replacing effective care with an unproven substitute. Readers who value these traditions can apply the same standard used throughout this book: examine the specific claim, the evidence behind it, and what regulators found.

\section{Homeopathy}
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=0pt,labelwidth=0pt,labelsep=0pt,itemindent=0pt]
\item[] \textbf{ALT-001.} \textbf{Myth:} Substances that cause symptoms in healthy people will cure the same symptoms in sick people.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Causing a symptom in a healthy person does not establish that a substance treats a disease with a similar symptom. \cite{ref469}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Look for controlled studies measuring outcomes in patients with the condition.
\item[] \textbf{ALT-002.} \textbf{Myth:} The more a homeopathic remedy is diluted, the stronger it becomes, because water remembers the original substance.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Dilution reduces the quantity of starting material. Some preparations are so dilute that a dose is unlikely to contain any of it. Therapeutic memory of water has not been demonstrated. \cite{ref469}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} A higher dilution number is not evidence of a stronger treatment.
\item[] \textbf{ALT-003.} \textbf{Myth:} Homeopathic nosodes can replace childhood vaccines.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Homeopathic nosodes have not demonstrated the disease-specific protection of recommended vaccines. \cite{ref469,ref314}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} A nosode does not count as a vaccine dose. Ask about catch-up vaccination if one was used instead.
\item[] \textbf{ALT-004.} \textbf{Myth:} Homeopathy must work because it helps babies and animals, who cannot experience placebo effects.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Natural recovery, other care, and observer expectations can all influence reports of improvement. The patient need not consciously expect a benefit for a caregiver's judgment to change. \cite{ref469}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} A blinded comparison helps distinguish a specific treatment effect from these influences.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Ayurvedic and Traditional Indian Claims}
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=0pt,labelwidth=0pt,labelsep=0pt,itemindent=0pt]
\item[] \textbf{ALT-005.} \textbf{Myth:} Metal-based Ayurvedic preparations are safe because traditional processing neutralizes their toxicity.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Analyses found lead, mercury, or arsenic in some traditionally prepared products. A description of processing cannot establish a safe exposure. \cite{ref115,ref116}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Show the product label and use history to a clinician if metal exposure is a concern.
\item[] \textbf{ALT-006.} \textbf{Myth:} Herbal products are automatically safe because they are natural, and cannot interact with medicines.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Herbs can alter the effects of medicines. St. John's wort, for example, can reduce the effectiveness of some prescription drugs. \cite{ref117,ref297}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Review herbal products with a pharmacist or clinician, especially before surgery or a medication change.
\item[] \textbf{ALT-007.} \textbf{Myth:} Drinking one's own urine cures infections, allergies, or chronic disease.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Drinking urine has no established therapeutic benefit for infections, allergies, or chronic disease. Its contents vary with illness, hydration, and medicines. \cite{ref393}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Use safe water for hydration and seek care for the underlying symptoms.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Meditation and Yoga}
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=0pt,labelwidth=0pt,labelsep=0pt,itemindent=0pt]
\item[] \textbf{ALT-008.} \textbf{Myth:} Yoga alone can cure diabetes, thyroid disease, or cancer without medication.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Yoga may support fitness and well-being. That does not establish a cure for diabetes, thyroid disease, or cancer. Type 1 diabetes requires insulin. \cite{ref118,ref271}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Integrate yoga with indicated treatment and review medication changes with the prescriber.
\item[] \textbf{ALT-009.} \textbf{Myth:} Meditation done properly means emptying the mind and stopping all thoughts.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} In mindfulness practice, noticing a thought and returning attention is part of the exercise. Meditation practices vary. \cite{ref470}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} A wandering mind does not mean the session has failed.
\item[] \textbf{ALT-010.} \textbf{Myth:} Mindfulness meditation can replace antidepressants and psychiatric treatment.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Structured mindfulness programs can help some people with depression. Their results do not establish that independent meditation can replace psychiatric treatment in general. \cite{ref136,ref470}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Discuss symptoms and prior episodes before changing an antidepressant or treatment plan.
\item[] \textbf{ALT-011.} \textbf{Myth:} Meditation has no side effects and is beneficial for absolutely everyone.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Some participants report anxiety, distress, or other unwanted effects. Estimates vary by practice and how researchers measure harm. \cite{ref134,ref470}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} If symptoms worsen repeatedly, pause or modify the practice and discuss the experience with a qualified instructor or clinician.
\item[] \textbf{ALT-012.} \textbf{Myth:} Meditation apps rapidly rewire the brain and deliver guaranteed transformation within weeks.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} A group-average change in symptoms or brain imaging cannot establish a guaranteed, lasting benefit from a particular app. \cite{ref136,ref470}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Check whether researchers tested the advertised program and followed participants after it ended.
\item[] \textbf{ALT-013.} \textbf{Myth:} Hot yoga detoxifies the body through profuse sweating.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Sweat cools the body and carries away water and salts. Heavy sweating during hot yoga has no established clinical detoxification benefit. \cite{ref303}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Stop and cool down if you feel faint, nauseated, or unwell during a heated class.
\item[] \textbf{ALT-014.} \textbf{Myth:} Yoga reliably cures chronic low back pain.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} In a trial of chronic nonspecific low back pain, yoga improved function compared with a self-care book and performed similarly to stretching. Outcomes varied among participants. \cite{ref135}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Choose movements suited to symptoms and ability. Persistent or worsening pain warrants reassessment.
\item[] \textbf{ALT-015.} \textbf{Myth:} Breathing exercises cure asthma and boost immunity to infection.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Breathing exercises may improve quality of life for some adults with asthma. Studies do not establish a cure for airway inflammation or protection against infection. \cite{ref288,ref179}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Use exercises as an addition to the asthma action plan and continue prescribed treatment.
\item[] \textbf{ALT-016.} \textbf{Myth:} Advanced yoga poses are safe for anyone flexible enough, and pain during practice means progress.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Flexibility alone cannot establish adequate strength, balance, or joint stability for an advanced pose. Pain can indicate an unsuitable load or position. \cite{ref137}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Use modifications and stop a movement that causes sharp or persistent pain.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Traditional Chinese Medicine}
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=0pt,labelwidth=0pt,labelsep=0pt,itemindent=0pt]
\item[] \textbf{ALT-017.} \textbf{Myth:} Aristolochia-containing Chinese herbs are safe traditional remedies for weight loss and joint pain.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Aristolochic acids can cause severe kidney injury and urinary tract cancers. Traditional use does not eliminate these hazards. \cite{ref119,ref120}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Avoid products containing Aristolochia. If you have taken one, show its label to a clinician and discuss follow-up.
\item[] \textbf{ALT-018.} \textbf{Myth:} Herbal weight-loss capsules contain only plant ingredients.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Some weight-loss products marketed as herbal have contained undeclared drugs. A botanical label cannot verify a batch's contents. \cite{ref121}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Check safety alerts for the exact product and mention it when seeking care for unexpected symptoms.
\item[] \textbf{ALT-019.} \textbf{Myth:} Rhino horn and tiger bone have proven medicinal value that justifies their use.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Reliable human evidence has not established that rhino horn or tiger bone treats the illnesses for which these products are promoted. \cite{ref122}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Seek condition-specific care. The rarity or price of a wildlife product is no measure of its medical value.
\item[] \textbf{ALT-020.} \textbf{Myth:} Acupuncture can cure infertility, allergies, asthma, or cancer.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Studies of symptom relief do not establish that acupuncture restores fertility, controls asthma, or eliminates cancer. \cite{ref123,ref471}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Ask which outcome improved in studies of the specific condition and continue indicated care.
\item[] \textbf{ALT-021.} \textbf{Myth:} Acupuncture provides meaningful, lasting relief for chronic back, neck, and joint pain.\newline
\textbf{Status:} Uncertain.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Trials show average relief in some chronic musculoskeletal pain conditions. Benefits are larger compared with no acupuncture than with sham acupuncture. Some benefit persists during follow-up, but size and duration vary. \cite{ref124,ref472}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Agree on a meaningful goal, such as easier walking, and reassess whether improvement justifies continuing.
\item[] \textbf{ALT-022.} \textbf{Myth:} Cupping draws toxins out of the body through the skin.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Suction can leave temporary marks through local effects on skin and blood vessels. Their color cannot identify a toxin or demonstrate its removal. \cite{ref473}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Cupping can cause burns or infection. Suspected toxic exposure needs assessment specific to the substance.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Energy, Manual, and Crystal Practices}
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=0pt,labelwidth=0pt,labelsep=0pt,itemindent=0pt]
\item[] \textbf{ALT-023.} \textbf{Myth:} Practitioners of energy therapies can feel and manipulate human energy fields to heal organs.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} In a blinded experiment, practitioners could not reliably detect which hand was near the investigator's hand. Evidence has not established that manipulating a proposed energy field heals organs. \cite{ref125}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Relaxation during a session cannot establish a change in organ disease.
\item[] \textbf{ALT-024.} \textbf{Myth:} Spinal manipulation cures infant colic, ear infections, and asthma.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Clinical evidence has not established spinal manipulation as a cure for infant colic, ear infections, or asthma. These conditions have different causes and treatments. \cite{ref126}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Seek pediatric assessment for persistent crying, ear pain, or breathing symptoms. Difficulty breathing warrants urgent attention.
\item[] \textbf{ALT-025.} \textbf{Myth:} Pressing specific zones of the feet treats the corresponding internal organs.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Reflexology assigns areas of the foot to internal organs. Controlled studies have not shown that pressing these points treats disease in the corresponding organs. \cite{ref127}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Foot massage may feel comfortable. A tender spot cannot diagnose an internal illness.
\item[] \textbf{ALT-026.} \textbf{Myth:} Crystals heal disease through their energy vibrations.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Crystals can have personal or ritual meaning. Clinical evidence has not established that their vibrations heal disease. \cite{ref286}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Use symptoms, examination, and appropriate tests to assess changes in illness.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Unproven Cancer and Detoxification Therapies}
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=0pt,labelwidth=0pt,labelsep=0pt,itemindent=0pt]
\item[] \textbf{ALT-027.} \textbf{Myth:} Apricot kernels or laetrile, marketed as vitamin B17, cure cancer safely.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Laetrile and amygdalin are marketed as vitamin B17, although neither is a recognized vitamin. Human trials have not demonstrated cancer treatment benefit. Oral preparations and apricot kernels can release cyanide. \cite{ref128,ref474}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Do not use them as cancer treatment. Seek poison advice promptly after a potentially toxic ingestion.
\item[] \textbf{ALT-028.} \textbf{Myth:} Intensive juicing regimes combined with coffee enemas cure cancer by detoxifying the liver.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} The Gerson regimen combines juices, dietary restrictions, and coffee enemas. Reliable evidence has not established a cancer cure. Enemas can cause serious fluid and electrolyte disturbances. \cite{ref129}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Discuss nutrition and unintended weight loss with the oncology team.
\item[] \textbf{ALT-029.} \textbf{Myth:} Black salve safely burns out skin cancer while sparing healthy tissue.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Black salve can destroy healthy and abnormal tissue alike. A scab does not establish that the entire cancer was removed. \cite{ref130}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Have suspicious lesions examined. Tell the clinician if salve was applied even if the original spot seems gone.
\item[] \textbf{ALT-030.} \textbf{Myth:} Chelation therapy cures autism by removing hidden mercury and other metals.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Chelation has specific uses for confirmed metal poisoning. Autism alone does not establish poisoning, and chelation has not been shown to cure autism. Inappropriate treatment can cause serious harm. \cite{ref131}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Seek exposure-specific assessment if there is a credible concern about lead or another metal.
\item[] \textbf{ALT-031.} \textbf{Myth:} Colloidal silver strengthens immunity and cures infections, including serious ones.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Swallowed colloidal silver has no established role in preventing or curing infections. It can cause lasting blue-gray skin discoloration and interfere with some medicines. \cite{ref132}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Medical uses of selected silver dressings do not justify drinking silver. Seek appropriate infection care.
\item[] \textbf{ALT-032.} \textbf{Myth:} Ear candles vacuum wax and toxins out of the ear canal safely.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Testing has not found that ear candles create suction sufficient to remove earwax. Residue can come from the candle itself. Burns and wax deposits in the ear are documented risks. \cite{ref133}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Have persistent fullness, pain, or hearing loss assessed instead of placing a burning device near the ear.
\end{enumerate}

\chapter{Asian Food and Medicine Myths}
Across Asia, food and medicine have shared a single vocabulary for millennia, and many remedies began as reasonable observations in their own time. This chapter examines specific food-as-medicine claims from China, Japan, Korea, and Southeast Asia, together with everyday beliefs that carry health consequences. India receives its own dedicated treatment elsewhere in this book. Three patterns recur. Foods praised as near-miraculous usually contain ordinary nutrients that the body digests like any other meal. Some traditional preparations carry genuine pharmacological activity along with genuine toxicity, which is precisely why regulated pharmaceutical versions exist. And the most serious harm rarely comes from enjoying a traditional food; it comes from substituting it for vaccination, anticoagulation, insulin, or cancer care. Enjoying cuisine and evaluating claims are compatible activities.

\section{Chinese Food as Medicine}
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=0pt,labelwidth=0pt,labelsep=0pt,itemindent=0pt]
\item[] \textbf{ASM-001.} \textbf{Myth:} Bird's nest soup builds immunity, beautifies skin, and justifies prices above silver.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Edible bird's nest is a traditional food. Studies of its composition or extracts do not establish broad immune protection or a reliable cosmetic benefit from eating the soup. \cite{ref138,ref475}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Choose it for culinary or cultural reasons if desired. Price is not a measure of medical benefit.
\item[] \textbf{ASM-002.} \textbf{Myth:} Shark fin soup promotes virility, longevity, and protection against cancer.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Reliable human evidence has not established that shark fin soup improves sexual function, prolongs life, or prevents cancer. Studies of extracts cannot establish these outcomes for the dish. \cite{ref476}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Persistent sexual symptoms and cancer prevention decisions warrant their own evidence-based assessment.
\item[] \textbf{ASM-003.} \textbf{Myth:} Bear bile treats fever and liver disease, and no substitute exists.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Bear bile contains ursodeoxycholic acid. Ursodiol, a standardized medicine, is used for specific conditions such as primary biliary cholangitis. This does not establish raw bile as a general liver or fever treatment. \cite{ref140,ref477}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} A diagnosis determines whether ursodiol is appropriate. Obtaining bile from a bear is unnecessary for that treatment.
\item[] \textbf{ASM-004.} \textbf{Myth:} Ginseng restores energy, immunity, fertility, and youth, working as a general panacea.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Studies of Asian ginseng have examined fatigue and other outcomes, with results that depend on the condition and preparation. They do not establish a general effect on youth, fertility, or immunity. \cite{ref141,ref478}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Review the exact product and purpose. Ginseng can cause insomnia and interact with medicines.
\item[] \textbf{ASM-005.} \textbf{Myth:} Cordyceps and reishi mushrooms cure cancer, kidney disease, and immune disorders.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Cordyceps and reishi are different fungi. Studies of extracts or use alongside treatment have not established that either cures cancer, kidney disease, or immune disorders. \cite{ref142,ref479}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Show supplement labels to the care team, especially during cancer treatment or after a transplant.
\item[] \textbf{ASM-006.} \textbf{Myth:} Eating an animal organ strengthens the corresponding human organ, so brains feed brains and hearts feed hearts.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Digestion breaks food into components the body can absorb. Eating heart or brain tissue does not direct its nutrients specifically to the corresponding human organ. \cite{ref209}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Choose foods for nutrient content and safety. Heart or neurological symptoms require their own assessment.
\item[] \textbf{ASM-007.} \textbf{Myth:} Snake wine and raw snake blood convey vitality and treat joint disease safely.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Reliable clinical evidence does not establish snake wine or raw snake blood as a treatment for joint disease. Alcohol does not guarantee that an animal product is safe to consume. \cite{ref480}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Avoid raw animal blood. Persistent joint pain or swelling deserves assessment.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Japanese Claims}
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=0pt,labelwidth=0pt,labelsep=0pt,itemindent=0pt]
\item[] \textbf{ASM-008.} \textbf{Myth:} Nattokinase dissolves blood clots and can replace prescription anticoagulants.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Nattokinase is an enzyme derived from fermented soybeans. A laboratory effect or a change in blood pressure does not show that a supplement treats clots or prevents stroke as effectively as prescribed anticoagulation. \cite{ref481}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Do not replace a prescribed anticoagulant with nattokinase. Discuss bleeding and clotting risks before combining products.
\item[] \textbf{ASM-009.} \textbf{Myth:} Drinking several glasses of water immediately upon waking cures diabetes, arthritis, constipation, and cancer.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Adequate fluid can help some people with constipation. No curative effect has been established for a fixed number of glasses on waking for diabetes, arthritis, or cancer. \cite{ref292,ref388}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Match fluid intake to your needs and any prescribed limits. Persistent symptoms require condition-specific care.
\item[] \textbf{ASM-010.} \textbf{Myth:} Eating the marketed Okinawan superfoods replicates Okinawan centenarian lifespans.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Observations about Okinawan centenarians cannot isolate one food from lifelong diet, activity, and social conditions. Buying a marketed ingredient has not been shown to reproduce a population's longevity. \cite{ref144}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Foods from Okinawan cuisine can be part of a varied diet. Product claims need evidence from people who consumed the product.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Korean Beliefs}
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=0pt,labelwidth=0pt,labelsep=0pt,itemindent=0pt]
\item[] \textbf{ASM-011.} \textbf{Myth:} Kimchi prevented SARS in 2003 and protects against respiratory viruses including COVID-19.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Comparisons of national SARS case counts cannot establish that kimchi prevented infection. Probiotic studies cannot be transferred directly to kimchi or COVID-19 prevention. \cite{ref145,ref482}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Enjoy kimchi as a food. Use established respiratory infection precautions rather than relying on fermented food for protection.
\item[] \textbf{ASM-012.} \textbf{Myth:} Sleeping in a closed room with an electric fan running can kill a healthy person overnight.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} An ordinary electric fan moves room air. It does not consume the oxygen in the room or cause suffocation simply because the door is closed. A different concern is relying on a fan alone during dangerous indoor heat. \cite{ref483}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Use a fan for comfort when conditions permit. During extreme heat, follow local heat guidance and move to a cooler place if you cannot stay cool.
\item[] \textbf{ASM-013.} \textbf{Myth:} Dog-meat tonics build stamina and male vigor beyond ordinary nutrition.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} A meat-based soup or tonic can supply energy and protein. Reliable human evidence has not established a special effect of dog meat on stamina or sexual function beyond ordinary nutrition. \cite{ref209,ref480}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Persistent fatigue or sexual difficulties merit assessment of their cause. A food's reputation cannot establish a therapeutic effect.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Southeast Asian Remedies}
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=0pt,labelwidth=0pt,labelsep=0pt,itemindent=0pt]
\item[] \textbf{ASM-014.} \textbf{Myth:} Tongkat ali root raises testosterone dramatically and cures male infertility.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Small studies of tongkat ali extracts have measured testosterone or other laboratory outcomes in selected adults. A change in a hormone measurement does not establish improved fertility, pregnancy, or live birth. \cite{ref150,ref484}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Seek evaluation for fertility concerns rather than assuming a supplement will correct them. Tell the clinician about any extract being used.
\item[] \textbf{ASM-015.} \textbf{Myth:} Kratom is a safe natural herb for energy and pain that causes no addiction.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} Kratom can have stimulant and opioid-like effects. Dependence and withdrawal have been reported, and human evidence for safe, effective pain treatment is limited. Product composition varies. \cite{ref485}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Discuss regular use with a clinician, particularly if stopping causes symptoms or if other sedating substances are involved.
\item[] \textbf{ASM-016.} \textbf{Myth:} Snakehead fish soup accelerates surgical wound healing far beyond ordinary nutrition.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} A small randomized study after cesarean delivery tested a snakehead fish extract. Some cosmetic ratings favored the extract, while pain and the wound evaluation score did not differ significantly. The study did not test soup against another equally nutritious meal. \cite{ref486}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} Fish can supply protein during recovery. The evidence does not justify promising unusually rapid healing or delaying assessment of wound redness, drainage, or fever.
\item[] \textbf{ASM-017.} \textbf{Myth:} Eating durian together with alcohol is a lethal combination.\newline
\textbf{Status:} False.\newline
\textbf{Fact:} A laboratory study found that durian extract inhibited an enzyme involved in alcohol metabolism. It did not measure illness or death in people eating durian with alcohol. Human evidence does not establish a routinely lethal interaction. \cite{ref290}\newline
\textbf{Implications:} The finding cannot be used to calculate a safe drinking limit. Heavy alcohol consumption can cause poisoning regardless of the meal.
